Individual visibility may arise from distinct material, geometry, topology, repeated units, appendage morphology, surface behaviour, hair/eye construction, body proportions, or other body-integrated consequences.

Shared physical causality arises when multiple concepts affect the same structure.

The compiler must seek both.

#### 5.3.26 Shared physical structures are preferred when compatible

Where two or more concepts can influence the same body system, the compiler must actively investigate a shared structure.

A shared structure may include:

- a torso region;
- ribcage architecture;
- spine;
- hip/lower-torso region;
- limb;
- joint;
- appendage root;
- appendage;
- cavity system;
- hair architecture;
- eye construction;
- surface material;
- repeated structural unit;
- silhouette boundary;
- body transition.

The shared structure must be physically coherent.

The goal is not to create a visual collage of separate motifs.

The goal is to create structures whose final form depends on multiple supplied concepts.

#### 5.3.27 Pairwise and higher-order interaction are mandatory analytical stages

For a concept set of cardinality n, the compiler must resolve:

- every supplied concept's active physical interpretation;
- all n(n−1)/2 distinct pairwise interactions;
- for n ≥ 3, the complete-set higher-order interaction;
- any intermediate higher-order interaction that genuinely determines the body.

For a three-concept command A + B + C, this produces A, B, C, A×B, A×C, B×C, and A×B×C. For two concepts, no nonexistent triple is invented. For four or more, the same generalized procedure expands to the actual set.

The complete-set higher-order interaction cannot be omitted when n ≥ 3.

A population may therefore contain structures whose final geometry, topology, material, scale, placement, attachment, face/head/hair behavior, or silhouette would not exist from any single concept alone.

This requirement is universal across creature, material, elemental, topology, behavioural, aesthetic, abstract, object-derived, and other concept categories.

#### 5.3.28 Generalized structural consequence test

For every major concept-bearing structure, ask:

1. What physical property of the supplied concept is represented?
2. Which body system expresses that property?
3. How does another supplied concept alter that same structure?
4. For three or more inputs, how do additional concepts alter or redirect it, and how does the complete set jointly determine the final construction?
5. What new structure exists because the concepts interact?
6. Is the result visible at macro scale?
7. Is the result physically integrated?
8. Does its architecture differ from the same concept set in the other four characters?

A structure that cannot answer the interaction questions is a weak candidate for primary materialization.

#### 5.3.29 Worked body-construction methodology

Consider a hypothetical feather-constituted female body: grouped feather matter forms intrinsic anatomy through articulated roots, directional overlap, and transitions between large and small structures. This worked example explains materialization without any external scene.

The worked construction demonstrates the following observable construction principles:

- major repeated units form large physical systems;
- those systems originate from and attach directly to the character;
- attachment occurs through substantial body regions rather than only through isolated decorative points;
- the repeated units alter the overall silhouette;
- the repeated units alter apparent mass distribution;
- the repeated units overlap and interlock;
- structures can extend through multiple body regions;
- structures vary in scale;
- structures vary in direction;
- structures vary in length;
- structures can be asymmetric;
- structures remain visually readable as part of the character;
- major concept-derived structures coexist with a clearly designed female body;
- the character remains readable without needing an external scene to explain the design;
- the macro silhouette remains organized despite substantial micro complexity.

These observations define methodology only.

They do not define a template.

They do not authorize copying the worked example’s exact anatomy, colours, head, appendages, costume, or identity.

#### 5.3.30 Worked construction principle: concept structures can dominate the silhouette

In the worked feather construction, large intrinsic feathered structures have substantial scale relative to the central body.

The important transferable principle is:

> **A concept-derived structural system may occupy a large fraction of the character silhouette when that is the strongest body-level expression of the concept.**

The compiler must therefore not under-size concept structures merely to preserve conventional humanoid proportions.

At the same time, the major shapes must remain legible.

The intended hierarchy is:

> **LARGE COHERENT STRUCTURES → CLEAR BODY READ → SECONDARY STRUCTURES → INTRICATE MICRODETAIL**

not a dense field of equally weighted small elements.

#### 5.3.31 Worked construction principle: repeated units become a coherent physical system

The worked construction’s feather units are not isolated symbols.

They form grouped systems.

Therefore, when a supplied concept has a repeated-unit visual property, the compiler should consider:

- grouping;
- clustering;
- directional families;
- nested scale;
- overlap;
- attachment hierarchy;
- local density;
- regional density differences;
- transitions between body regions;
- integration with the main silhouette.

This is a generalized materialization mechanism.

It can be adapted to any supplied concept whose visual properties support repeated structural units.

#### 5.3.32 Worked construction principle: multiple attachment regions

The worked construction demonstrates that a concept-derived system can connect to multiple major regions of the same character.

The transferable rule is:

> **When the concept supports a distributed body system, investigate multiple integrated attachment regions rather than a single default locus.**

Attachment architecture must therefore be selected as part of the character's specific design.

The same locus must not become an automatic population default.

#### 5.3.33 Worked construction principle: scale hierarchy

The worked construction contains multiple scales of concept-derived structures.

- primary concept structures establish the macro;
- medium structures establish internal organization;
- small structures reinforce transitions;
- micro-detail enriches surfaces.

This is compatible with simple macro / intricate micro.

It is not a justification for macro saturation.

#### 5.3.34 Worked construction principle: asymmetry is structurally useful

The worked construction demonstrates that asymmetric distribution can make the concept-derived body architecture more specific.

> **Asymmetry must be used as a design mechanism where it creates a meaningful difference in structure, not as random noise.**

Asymmetry may affect:

- appendage count;
- appendage size;
- appendage origin;
- appendage orientation;
- body topology;
- repeated-unit density;
- material distribution;
- cavity distribution;
- local silhouette;
- hair architecture;
- eye treatment;
- other body systems.

Each character may resolve asymmetry differently.

The same asymmetry pattern must not become a population template.

#### 5.3.35 Worked construction principle: concept structures interact with anatomy

A major concept-derived system should modify how the body is read.

The worked construction demonstrates that large appendages and repeated structures interact with:

- shoulders;
- torso;
- limbs;
- silhouette;
- mass distribution;
- local surface architecture.

> **Concept structures must modify the reading of the body rather than merely sit beside it.**

This applies to appendages, cavities, materials, repeated units, altered surface topology, and any other relevant body system.

#### 5.3.36 Generalized operational example: creature-derived morphology

A creature-derived concept may alter:

- cranial geometry;
- ears;
- jaw;
- teeth;
- neck;
- ribcage;
- limb joints;
- claws or distal structure;
- tail architecture;
- wing architecture;
- dorsal structures;
- body substrate;
- internal supports;
- silhouette.

The exact selection, count, origin, scale, geometry, material, asymmetry, and interaction must be resolved from the complete concept combination for each character.

A familiar iconic creature feature is not automatically the best solution.

The five characters must be permitted to express the creature concept through materially different body architectures.

#### 5.3.37 Generalized operational example: aperture / discontinuity / absence concepts

A concept whose physical properties support discontinuity, absence, penetration, separation, void, aperture, cavity, rupture, or related topology must be considered at the body level.

Possible body-level manifestations include:

- cavities;
- through-openings;
- recessed openings;
- nested openings;
- interrupted structural planes;
- branching internal channels;
- missing-volume topology;
- split structural regions;
- perforated body regions;
- discontinuous plates;
- aperture systems integrated into appendage roots;
- openings integrated into load-bearing structures;
- asymmetrical void distributions.

The selected mechanism must vary by character.

Its body locus must not converge into one repeated body region merely because that location is easy to render.

The aperture system must interact with the other supplied concepts.

#### 5.3.38 Generalized operational rule: nonuniformity / irregularity concepts

A concept associated with instability, irregularity, nonuniformity, disorder, variation, or related physical properties must be translated into structural nonuniformity.

Potential mechanisms include:

- irregular branching;
- variable segment lengths;
- unequal bilateral structures;
- competing local orientations resolved into one coherent anatomy;
- nonuniform appendage counts;
- abrupt but coherent transitions;
- multiple growth directions;
- uneven cavity distribution;
- asymmetric joints;
- irregular structural plates;
- heterogeneous interlocking substrate;
- non-repeating topology.

The implementation must remain part of the body.

Nonuniformity must be aesthetically authored.

Random noise is not a substitute for design.

#### 5.3.39 Generalized operational example: elemental or force concepts

An elemental or force concept must be considered as a body property first.

Potential body-level channels include:

- substrate;
- internal structure;
- surface geometry;
- appendage material;
- body topology;
- material transitions;
- hair construction;
- eye construction;
- markings;
- joint architecture;
- silhouette.

The exact implementation depends on the concept combination.

A phenomenon normally depicted around a character must not become the primary carrier when the same concept can be materially expressed through the body.

#### 5.3.40 Generalized operational example: behavioural, aesthetic, cultural, or style concepts

A behavioural, aesthetic, cultural, or style concept must be translated into character-intrinsic visual construction.

Potential channels include:

- face geometry;
- eye geometry;
- brows;
- mouth construction;
- hair architecture;
- hair colour;
- character-identity finishing, including optional cosmetics where selected;
- skin treatment;
- markings;
- nails;
- body proportions;
- surface finishing;
- colour relationships;
- material relationships;
- silhouette;
- controlled expression where the view remains neutral.

Such a concept must not be reduced to a pose, a costume, a detached symbol, or an imported associated identity.

The concept must contribute to the authored female character itself.

#### 5.3.41 Direct-property derivation for nonmaterial concepts — HARD

For every nonmaterial primary concept, explicitly record the direct properties chosen for visual translation and the body systems those properties change. The route remains concept property → physical consequence. Familiar associative imagery is not part of the derivation.
#### 5.3.42 Nonmaterial concepts must deform shared structures — HARD

A nonmaterial primary concept passes only when it changes at least one shared structure that also depends on another primary concept. Identity finishing may support the reading, but the fusion must remain physically interdependent inside the body.
#### 5.3.43 Neutral-presentation preservation for nonmaterial concepts — HARD

Nonmaterial concept embodiment must survive the standard true-front neutral-standing analytical view. Pose, expression, narrative context, and page treatment are not counted as primary evidence. Renderer serialization states only the frozen visible construction.
#### 5.3.44 Concept-specific examples are explanatory, not templates

Every concept-specific example in this specification exists to clarify a generalized mechanism.

The compiler must:

1. apply the generalized rule;
2. extract the directly useful properties of the actual supplied concept;
3. fuse them with the other supplied concepts;
4. design a new body architecture;
5. use the example only as a reasoning aid;
6. reject template copying.

An example therefore explains **how to think**, not **what shape to repeat**.

#### 5.3.45 No concept-to-feature partitioning

The five characters must not use a repeated architecture of:

> concept A occupies region X
> concept B occupies region Y
> concept C occupies region Z.

That is additive partitioning rather than fusion.

The required architecture is:

> **A × B × C → INTERDEPENDENT BODY ARCHITECTURE**

A single body system may visibly carry several concepts simultaneously. Different interactions may also be distributed across different systems when the complete body remains one fused construction.

The same concept may influence one or several body systems depending on the strongest solution. No concept receives a fixed anatomical territory, and no numerical quota of shared systems is imposed.

The exact mapping and interaction architecture must vary across the five characters.

#### 5.3.46 No repeated concept recipe across the population

For each character, record:

- primary fusion locus;
- secondary fusion loci;
- concept-carrier distribution;
- pairwise interaction mechanisms;
- higher-order interaction mechanism;
- topology mechanism;
- material mechanism;
- appendage mechanism;
- aperture mechanism where applicable;
- asymmetry mechanism;
- silhouette mechanism.

Compare these records across all five characters.

A repeated recipe is insufficient even when the visible parameters differ.

The compiler must rebuild until the five records represent materially different physical solutions.

#### 5.3.47 Character-specific concept prominence

Each character must have its own way of making the supplied concepts prominent.

Prominence may come from:

- one dominant structural system;
- several interdependent major systems;
- a large silhouette-defining architecture;
- distributed body topology;
- material architecture;
- appendage architecture;
- internal negative-space architecture;
- face/head/hair/character-identity construction;
- combinations of the above.

The prominence mechanism itself must contribute to differentiation.

#### 5.3.48 Concept prominence must survive simplified viewing

At normal sheet viewing scale, the major concept-bearing body systems must still be apparent.

Zooming may reveal additional detail.

Zooming must not be required to discover the primary concept fusion.

The normal-scale read must communicate:

- the female character identity;
- the body architecture;
- the dominant concept-derived structures;
- the broad material architecture;
- the major silhouette;
- the difference between the five characters.

#### 5.3.49 Body-carrier removal audit

Perform the following conceptual removal sequence:

1. remove page decoration;
2. remove visual field information;
3. remove any presentation-only content;
4. inspect the remaining character;
5. determine whether the supplied concepts are still materially encoded.

The valid result is:

> **THE BODY STILL CONTAINS THE CONCEPTS.**

If the concept identity weakens because something outside the character was removed, the materialization solution is inadequate.

#### 5.3.50 Concept-combination recognition audit

For every character, ask:

> **Could a viewer infer the complete supplied combination from this body without being told which external effect, prop, scene, or symbol was intended?**

The answer must be yes.

The explanation must come from the body.

The body must not require an explanatory legend.

#### 5.3.51 Whole-body transformation requirement

Concept combination materialization must affect the body as a whole design system.

This does not mean every square centimetre must contain the same visible treatment.

It means the complete body must have been authored under the combined concept constraints.

The compiler must therefore inspect:

- head;
- face;
- eyes;
- hair;
- neck;
- shoulders;
- torso;
- chest architecture;
- waist;
- hip/lower-torso region;
- hips;
- limbs;
- joints;
- hands;
- feet;
- substrate;
- surface structures;
- internal topology;
- appendages;
- silhouette.

A design that leaves most of the body generic and places the concept on one local region is insufficient.

#### 5.3.52 Concept-derived structures must be integrated before decoration

Primary body construction is resolved first.

Surface decoration is then derived from and aligned with that construction.

The compiler must not use decorative motifs as a substitute for structural concept materialization.

Decorative elements may reinforce a valid body architecture.

They cannot establish that architecture by themselves.

#### 5.3.53 Structural prominence versus visual noise

When increasing concept materialization because a concept is not yet unmistakably or materially readable, the compiler must first enlarge, refine, or improve the integration of major structures.

It must not first increase the number of tiny marks, particles, fragments, sparks, seams, surface symbols, or micro-elements. Once concept completeness and the required interactions already pass, unused anatomical space is not a reason to continue escalating concept coverage. Additional manifestations are added only when they strengthen the character.

The correct escalation sequence, only while stronger embodiment is genuinely required, is:

1. stronger body architecture;
2. stronger major concept structures;
3. clearer interactions;
4. stronger silhouette;
5. clearer material boundaries;
6. medium-scale supporting structures;
7. intricate micro-detail.

#### 5.3.54 Macro saturation guard

A character fails the macro hierarchy when:

- too many unrelated major shapes compete at the same scale;
- the body silhouette cannot be read quickly;
- concept structures overlap without hierarchy;
- appendages form an undifferentiated mass;
- cavities or topology merge into visual noise;
- facial identity disappears at sheet scale;
- later characters become harder to read because their complexity is uncontrolled.

The correction is architectural simplification, not reduction of concept embodiment.

The compiler must reorganize major structures so that concept completeness, interaction evidence, and recognizability remain strong while the number of dominant macro masses remains controlled. Density itself is not a target.

#### 5.3.55 Intricate micro-detail remains mandatory

The macro saturation guard does not authorize low detail.

Once the macro architecture is stable, micro-detail must be developed around it.

Micro-detail may include:

- fine contour variation;
- material texture;
- repeated units;
- small structural seams;
- local markings;
- fine hair strands;
- small highlight shapes;
- small material transitions;
- intricate edge organization;
- subtle eye detailing;
- optional cosmetic finishing where selected.

The micro layer must reinforce the authored character.

It must never redefine the macro.

#### 5.3.56 COMPLETE-BODY CONCEPT MATERIALIZATION MUST BE VISUALLY PROMINENT — HARD / ABSOLUTE

A concept is not sufficiently materialized merely because it technically changes the body.

Its materialization must have enough **scale, structural role, material distinction, integration, and visual emphasis in the appropriate carriers** that the concept materially contributes to the character's immediate identity. Repetition and broad distribution are available mechanisms, not universal requirements.

Every primary concept must have at least one major unmistakable manifestation readable at macro or strong meso scale, plus the required interaction evidence defined elsewhere. A concept may additionally propagate into intricate micro-detail. Micro-detail alone never establishes completion.

A concept that is physically present only as tiny markings, isolated eye details, minor colour accents, one small token feature, or subtle texture is insufficient when the concept permits and requires stronger body-level manifestation.

The compiler MUST actively search for the **strongest necessary body consequence**, not the largest possible quantity of consequences. Once concept completeness and all interactions pass, aesthetic economy governs further addition.

The complete concept combination should remain legible at ordinary sheet scale and after micro-detail is visually compressed, without requiring every body region to carry equal visual intensity.

#### 5.3.57 CONCEPTS MUST MATERIALIZE THE BODY, NOT DECORATE IT — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The complete supplied concept set is the actual body-design input.

The body MUST be constructed as a consequence of the fused concepts.

The workflow MUST NOT construct a generic female body first and then attach concept manifestations afterward.

The correct order is:

> **COMPLETE CONCEPT PROPERTY DECOMPOSITION**
>
> →
>
> **COMBINED PHYSICAL TRANSFORMATION LOGIC**
>
> →
>
> **BODY ARCHITECTURE**
>
> →
>
> **BODY SUBSTRATE**
>
> →
>
> **INTERNAL TOPOLOGY**
>
> →
>
> **APPENDAGE ARCHITECTURE**
>
> →
>
> **FACE / HAIR / EYES**
>
> →
>
> **MATERIAL AND COLOUR ARCHITECTURE**
>
> →
>
> **CHARACTER-SPECIFIC IDENTITY FINISHING**

The incorrect order is:

> generic female body
>
> +
>
> concept decorations
>
> +
>
> external concept effects.

This rule applies to every concept category.

#### 5.3.58 UNIVERSAL CONCEPT CATEGORY TRANSLATION — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Every supplied concept must first be decomposed into directly usable visual properties.

No category is exempt.

##### 5.3.58.1 Creature / organism / animal-derived concepts

Translate the supplied creature identity into character-level physical systems such as:

- cranial geometry;
- jaw geometry;
- dental geometry;
- ocular structure;
- ear structure;
- integument;
- fur/scale/plate organization;
- limb architecture;
- joint architecture;
- claws or altered distal structures;
- tail architecture;
- wing architecture;
- fin architecture;
- membrane architecture;
- horn/crest architecture;
- internal support;
- body substrate;
- silhouette.

The exact systems used must depend on the complete concept fusion.

A creature concept does not prescribe a fixed appendage template.

##### 5.3.58.2 Material / substance / mineral / crystalline concepts

Translate material identity into:

- body substrate;
- tissue architecture;
- surface geometry;
- structural density;
- transparency/translucency/opacity;
- reflective behaviour expressed through illustration;
- internal inclusions;
- segmentation;
- fracture-like topology where appropriate;
- planar or curved structural organization;
- material transitions;
- appendage construction;
- hair/eye integration;
- silhouette consequences.

The material must become part of what the body physically is.

##### 5.3.58.3 Fluid / water / liquid concepts

Translate fluid identity into:

- body-continuity patterns;
- flowing anatomical structures;
- integrated channels;
- translucent body regions;
- fluid-derived appendages;
- internal flow topology;
- altered surface continuity;
- body-mass transitions;
- hair architecture;
- eye structure;
- membrane structures;
- distributed fluid-derived morphology.

Do not rely on an external liquid system to carry the concept.

##### 5.3.58.4 Weather / atmospheric / environmental-physical concepts

Translate them into bodily physical consequences:

- body-surface structure;
- branching internal patterns;
- directional anatomical structures;
- integrated particulate-free morphology;
- altered substrate;
- appendages;
- topology;
- material transitions;
- silhouette;
- hair and eye structures;
- other directly embodied systems.

The environment remains analytically empty in Phase 1.

##### 5.3.58.5 Energy / force / field / radiation concepts

Translate them into:

- internal body architecture;
- directional structural patterns;
- channels;
- branching systems;
- integrated cavities;
- altered joints;
- body-substrate organization;
- appendage architecture;
- ocular architecture;
- material transitions;
- silhouette mechanisms.

The energy or force must be physically embodied.

Detached rays, floating fields, emitted beams, generic surrounding halos, or environmental displays are not substitutes for body materialization. A finite spatially separated structure remains eligible only when it is genuine certified body-bound identity morphology under §0.0, not a surrounding effect.

##### 5.3.58.6 Spatial / dimensional / topology concepts

- altered spatial continuity;
- internal cavities;
- nested spaces;
- discontinuous body planes;
- impossible-yet-coherent geometry;
- non-Euclidean-looking body transitions rendered as 2D structure;
- integrated openings;
- altered appendage roots;
- unusual internal architecture;
- asymmetric body continuity;
- silhouette discontinuities.

The result remains a female character.

The character does not become an abstract diagram or environmental spatial effect.

##### 5.3.58.7 Temporal / process / state concepts

- visible sequential structural variation distributed through the body;
- altered material states across connected anatomy;
- repeated or evolving body structures;
- structural transitions when visually warranted;
- hair/eye variations;
- appendage-state differences;
- internal segmentation;
- material transitions;
- directional structural flow.

No lore, biography, or chronology interpretation is introduced.

Only visible physical design is encoded.

##### 5.3.58.8 Abstract / quality / behavioural / conceptual concepts

Translate the supplied concept into directly visualizable character-design properties using the concept's explicit visual implications and no invented narrative context.

Possible carrier classes include:

- facial geometry;
- eye design;
- eyebrow geometry;
- mouth geometry;
- hair architecture;
- body silhouette;
- posture-compatible static silhouette;
- body proportion;
- appendage architecture;
- material structure;
- colour architecture;
- ornamental body geometry;
- surface organization;
- asymmetry.

Because Phase 1 uses neutral standing, behavioural concepts MUST NOT depend on action poses.

The visual identity must be expressed through the body, face/head, hair, eyes, materials, colour/value architecture, proportions, silhouette, markings/pigmentation where selected, optional cosmetics where selected, and other directly observable character systems.

##### 5.3.58.9 Object / structure / tool / architectural-form concepts

- body geometry;
- structural frames;
- internal supports;
- cavities;
- segmentation;
- repeated structural systems;
- appendages;
- joints;
- material architecture;
- silhouette;
- surface geometry.

The object itself must not be held, worn, placed beside the character, or used as a scene prop to carry the concept.

#### 5.3.59 CONCEPT CO-DOMINANCE IS REQUIRED WITHOUT UNIFORM SATURATION — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Every supplied primary concept remains equally necessary to the finished fusion. No concept may become the whole design while another collapses into a minor decoration or label.

Co-dominance concerns **conceptual necessity and unmistakable material presence**, not equal surface area, equal number of carriers, equal brightness, or equal visual intensity in every region. Different concepts may lead different local systems as long as the others remain strongly integrated through their own signatures and shared structures.

The compiler must explicitly identify for each concept:

- at least one major unmistakable physical consequence;
- at least one supporting consequence where meaningful;
- at least one shared consequence with another primary concept;
- at least one higher-order consequence when three or more concepts are supplied.

The exact systems and relative visual emphasis may vary between characters. One concept may dominate a local region, another may determine a substrate behavior, and another may determine topology, while all remain inseparable from the same complete body construction.

No primary concept may disappear merely because another produced a visually spectacular structure. Conversely, no additional structure should be added merely to make the concepts occupy equal amounts of anatomy.

#### 5.3.60 CROSS-CONCEPT STRUCTURES MUST HAVE MAJOR OR STRONG MESO-SCALE CONSEQUENCE — HARD / ABSOLUTE

A cross-concept structure is not a tiny motif in which multiple concepts are superficially represented.

A valid cross-concept structure must materially alter one or more of the following: size, structural role, geometry, topology, material, attachment, silhouette, proportion, appendage construction, internal architecture, face/head system, hair system, or another identity-bearing physical system.

Suitable shared systems may include an appendage whose root and material are jointly determined by multiple concepts; a torso system whose proportions and internal architecture arise from their interaction; a limb/joint system with shared topology; a cranial system whose structures arise from the same fusion; a whole-body substrate jointly determined by several concepts; or another equally substantial solution.

The compiler must actively search for **meaningful shared construction**, not a numerical quota of separate shared objects. One strong integrated system may carry several cross-concept consequences. Three separate isolated feature groups are not equivalent.

#### 5.3.61 HIGHER-ORDER FUSION MUST BE STRUCTURALLY CONSEQUENTIAL — HARD / ABSOLUTE

For three or more supplied concepts, the complete combination must create one or more structures whose final state depends on all supplied concepts acting together.

Higher-order fusion must be strong enough that removing one concept would require a meaningful redesign of the shared construction. It may propagate through multiple regions or systems when that creates a stronger character, but multiple-region propagation is **not** a mandatory saturation quota.

The designer must balance higher-order completeness with aesthetic economy:

- all supplied concepts remain materially necessary;
- the higher-order construction is clearly readable at macro or strong meso scale;
- additional propagation is used only when it strengthens recognizability, physical coherence, silhouette, material identity, character appeal, or interaction completeness;
- unused anatomical space is not a reason to repeat the fusion;
- a quiet region may remain visually quiet while still belonging to a body whose complete architecture was authored under the fused concepts.

The exact higher-order architecture must vary between characters. Reusing one generic triple-fusion structure with altered styling is invalid.

#### 5.3.62 APPENDAGE DIVERSITY MUST BE RADICAL AND AESTHETICALLY INTENTIONAL — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Whenever appendages are present because the major recognizable vocabulary or resolved fusion requires them, the population must exploit the available design space aggressively. Supporting or optional appendages may be omitted when omission strengthens the design, but required major characteristics may not be omitted for differentiation.

Vary, as applicable:

- number of appendages;
- number of pairs;
- single versus paired distribution;
- bilateral versus unilateral distribution;
- origin;
- origin depth;
- attachment zone;
- axial versus peripheral origin;
- cranial versus dorsal versus lateral versus hip/lower-torso versus limb-root origin;
- direction;
- angle;
- curvature;
- segmentation;
- articulation;
- branching;
- taper;
- length;
- thickness;
- span;
- mass;
- internal support;
- membrane structure;
- surface structure;
- material;
- colour;
- integration depth;
- asymmetry;
- interaction with the rest of the body;
- contribution to silhouette;
- interaction with cavities;
- interaction with the concept fusion.

The same appendage type may recur as a concept-visible principle while its actual implementation changes drastically.

For example, the population may contain different numbers of wing pairs, different wing origins, different wing geometries, different wing materials, different supporting structures, and different interactions with the torso.

The presence of a repeated concept does not authorize a repeated appendage design.

Appendages must be authored as part of the entire character.

#### 5.3.63 FACE QUALITY MUST REACH MAIN-CHARACTER LEVEL — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Every character requires a face capable of supporting the entire character identity at the same quality level as the body.

The compiler MUST actively develop:

- face shape;
- forehead structure;
- brow shape;
- eye geometry;
- eye spacing;
- eyelid structure;
- iris design;
- pupil design;
- eye highlights;
- eyebrow shape;
- nose structure;
- mouth shape;
- lip geometry;
- cheek structure;
- jaw structure;
- chin structure;
- ear design;
- face framing;
- hairline;
- facial contour;
- eye expression at neutral standing;
- character-specific identity finishing, with cosmetic details only where deliberately selected.

Faces must not converge into one shared anime face with different hair colours.

The five characters may share the same broad art style.

They may not share the same facial identity.

The face must be developed enough to survive full-sheet viewing and to remain legible as an independent character.

A face that is only convincing after extreme magnification fails the quality gate.

#### 5.3.64 CHARACTER-IDENTITY COMPLETION IS REQUIRED FOR EVERY CHARACTER — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Each character must be aesthetically and recognizably complete enough to function as a principal anime/manhwa/gacha character.

Identity completion includes the deliberate authorship of the underlying face/head geometry, eyes, hair architecture, material transitions around identity-bearing regions, pigmentation or markings where selected, and any other finishing systems that make this exact character memorable.

Conventional cosmetics are **optional tools**, not mandatory features. Eyeliner, lashes, eyeshadow, blush, lip colour, nail finishing, decorative facial markings, or similar beauty treatments may be used when they improve the character. A no-makeup character can pass fully when her face, eyes, hair, proportions, materials, and other identity systems are strong and complete.

Identity finishing must enhance an already independent character. It must not be used as a substitute for body differentiation, concept fusion, facial geometry, or population independence.

**REQUIRED:**

> **independent body + independent fusion + independent silhouette + independent face/head + independent character identity**

**INVALID:**

> **same body/face + different makeup, markings, or hair colour**.

#### 5.3.65 BODY IDENTITY AND CONCEPT IDENTITY MUST BE SIMULTANEOUSLY READABLE — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The viewer should be able to determine two things from the image itself:

1. this is a distinct female anime/manhwa character;
2. this character is a distinct physical materialization of the complete supplied concept combination.

Neither identity may depend on labels.

Neither identity may depend on external scene information.

Neither identity may depend on a separate concept icon.

Neither identity may depend on pose.

Neither identity may depend on a verbal explanation.

The body must carry the concept fusion.

The face/head, eyes, hair, materials, colour/value architecture, markings and optional cosmetics, and silhouette must complete the character identity.

The full result must therefore satisfy:

> **CHARACTER IDENTITY + CONCEPT IDENTITY + CONCEPT FUSION + AESTHETIC COMPLETION**

simultaneously.

#### 5.3.66 COLOUR ARCHITECTURE MUST BE INTELLIGENTLY AUTHORED ACROSS THE POPULATION — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The population is not restricted to monochrome and is not required to distribute a different numeric colour count to every character.

A character may be monochrome, near-monochrome, bichromatic, trichromatic, tetracolour, multi-colour, strongly polychromatic, rainbow-oriented, or use another coherent palette architecture. Several characters may legitimately use the same approximate number of hue families when their actual colour architecture is substantially different.

The exact colour count is an available design property, **not a population quota**.

The compiler MUST compare actual colour architecture across:

- dominant and subordinate hue families;
- value hierarchy;
- saturation hierarchy;
- warm/cool organization;
- local contrast;
- material-specific colour behavior;
- location of the darkest and lightest masses;
- location of strongest saturation;
- hair/body relationship;
- eye/body relationship;
- appendage/body relationship;
- cavity/interior versus exterior relationship where applicable;
- focal accents;
- distribution across the silhouette.

An initial sheet group of five pale bodies with dark appendages and one bright accent fails even if the nominal hue counts differ. Several restrained palettes may pass when their distribution, material behavior, temperature, value, saturation, and focal hierarchy are genuinely different.

Colour is an identity-completion and compositional system. It never substitutes for structural differentiation.

#### 5.3.67 MATERIAL ARCHITECTURE MUST ALSO VARY — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Different characters must not merely receive different colours over the same material construction.

Vary, when visually justified:

- substrate type;
- substrate continuity;
- hard versus soft structural expression;
- smooth versus segmented structure;
- glossy versus matte illustration language;
- translucent versus opaque regions;
- reflective versus diffuse regions;
- crystalline versus fibrous versus layered versus membranous versus organic versus mineral-like construction;
- internal material organization;
- material boundaries;
- transition behaviour;
- interaction between materials.

The body remains one coherent physical construction.

Material diversity must reinforce the character's independent aesthetic design.

#### 5.3.67A MATERIAL DISCRIMINABILITY FLOOR — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Every materially different constituent must look materially different at ordinary sheet scale. Do not allow the renderer to collapse skin/soft substrate, keratin, scale, membrane, mineral, crystal, powdery/porous matter, fluid-like matter, bone-like matter, metal-like matter, void/negative topology, or other resolved constituents into one universal glossy fantasy surface.

For every significant material, resolve a distinct combination of:

- edge character;
- shadow softness/hardness;
- highlight shape and intensity;
- gloss/matte response;
- opacity/translucency;
- granularity/porosity/fiber/fracture logic;
- thickness cues;
- internal pattern or layering;
- local contrast;
- boundary and transition behavior.

At least the material hierarchy must remain readable even when hue differences are visually compressed. A pale mineral, pale scale, pale skin-like substrate, and pale membrane cannot all be distinguished only by colour.

Within each five-character sheet group, a shared concept-derived material is not a preset shader. Its exact unit scale, grain, fracture, lamination, translucency, density, boundary logic, and anatomical organization are independently authored for each character when the fusion permits variation.

A material-discriminability failure blocks rendering even when the palette is attractive.

#### 5.3.68 UNUSUALNESS IS PERMITTED; PREMIUM APPEAL REMAINS MANDATORY — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The workflow allows extreme, unusual, nonstandard and physically impossible character constructions.

This freedom exists to enlarge the design space.

It is not permission to create incoherent, accidental, crude, malformed, unreadable, unfinished, or technically clever but aesthetically undesirable bodies.

An unusual design must still have:

- coherent structure;
- deliberate hierarchy;
- intentional silhouette;
- refined proportions;
- controlled asymmetry;
- readable anatomy;
- coherent attachment;
- meaningful material transitions;
- deliberate concept fusion;
- developed face;
- developed hair;
- deliberate character-identity finishing;
- professional rendering.

The design may be strange.

The design may be extreme.

The design may violate normal anatomy.

The design must still look intentionally designed.

#### 5.3.69 ASYMMETRY MUST BE DESIGNED, NOT APPLIED AS A FILTER — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Asymmetry must arise from the character construction.

Do not create five symmetric bodies and then apply random asymmetry.

Vary:

- left/right structural balance;
- appendage count;
- appendage origin;
- appendage scale;
- body topology;
- cavity distribution;
- limb architecture;
- material distribution;
- hair masses;
- facial details;
- silhouette;
- internal structures.

Asymmetry must have structural consequences.

The population must contain multiple asymmetry grammars.

A repeated “one large structure on the left, one small structure on the right” recipe is not sufficient.

A character may remain approximately bilateral if that is an intentional result of its construction.

The population as a whole must not converge on one asymmetry recipe.

#### 5.3.70 MACRO READABILITY MUST CONTROL STRUCTURAL COMPLEXITY — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The purpose of intricate micro-detail is to enrich the resolved macro design.

The compiler MUST first create a readable macro.

The renderer must then add micro-detail inside the macro.

Macro structure must remain readable before micro-detail is inspected.

The following visual hierarchy is mandatory:

1. overall silhouette and immediately dominant visual hook;
2. face, head, eyes, and hair as a primary character-identity focal region;
3. major body architecture and proportion system;
4. dominant concept-fusion structure or small set of dominant fusion structures;
5. major appendage, cavity, and topology architecture;
6. major material, value, and colour masses and their controlled contrasts;
7. supporting concept-derived structures;
8. secondary structural detail;
9. fine topology and material texture;
10. micro-surface detail.

This hierarchy is a design-priority hierarchy rather than a rigid instruction that every viewer must look at exactly the same point first. The face and head should normally remain one of the strongest focal regions because the character must be recognizable and desirable as an individual, not merely as a body carrying unusual anatomy. A deliberate character-specific hook may momentarily dominate the first read, but it must lead back into a developed face/head identity rather than bury it beneath structural complexity.

If levels 8–10 visually overpower the silhouette, face/head identity, major body architecture, or dominant fusion structures, the design fails.

High complexity is allowed.

Uncontrolled complexity is not.

A character must never become a field of spikes, fragments, seams, petals, feathers, shards, cables, reflections, or micro-patterns whose aggregate density erases the body architecture.

#### 5.3.71 CONCEPT MATERIALIZATION MUST BE RECALCULATED FOR EACH CHARACTER — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Primary materialization must be solved independently for every one of the fifteen initial characters after that candidate's complete Resolved Primary Concept Set has been frozen. Where slots are fixed, the same fixed concepts remain active across all fifteen; where selectors/wildcards are used, candidate-specific resolved leaves may legitimately differ.

The compiler must not build one universal materialization template and then instantiate it across a sheet or the full population.

For each character, the compiler must independently determine:

- which body systems are most transformed;
- which body systems are secondarily transformed;
- where the strongest fusion occurs;
- where the concepts intersect;
- how the substrate changes;
- how topology changes;
- how appendage roots change;
- how major appendages change;
- how silhouette changes;
- how asymmetry changes;
- how material architecture changes;
- how the face/hair/eyes participate;
- how identity finishing completes the identity.

Only after all fifteen independent resolutions exist may the three checkpoint-strength within-sheet comparisons and the global fifteen-character collision review be completed.

#### 5.3.72 THE COMPLETE CONCEPT SET MUST REMAIN VISIBLE AFTER DIFFERENTIATION — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Independent character construction must not weaken concept recognition.

The five solutions must differ strongly.

The concepts must remain equally necessary and unmistakable. They do not need equal surface area, equal brightness, equal carrier count, or identical visual intensity.

**REQUIRED:**

> **FIVE DIFFERENT BODIES + FIVE DIFFERENT FUSION ARCHITECTURES + EACH CANDIDATE'S FROZEN COMPLETE RESOLVED PRIMARY SET + SAME PRIMARY-CONCEPT CO-DOMINANCE RULE**

**INVALID:**

> **FIVE DIFFERENT BODIES + ONE DOMINANT CONCEPT + WEAK REMAINING CONCEPTS**

Concept differentiation between characters therefore changes the implementation.

It does not reduce the concept's strength.

#### 5.3.73 FORMAT MUST BE SERIALIZED AS A RIGID GEOMETRIC CONTRACT — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Phase 1 format is not inferred from the phrase `character sheet`.

The renderer receives:

> **ONE CONTINUOUS 16:9 LANDSCAPE CANVAS → FIVE LEFT-TO-RIGHT REGIONS → ONE LARGE FULL-BODY TRUE-FRONT NEUTRAL-STANDING CHARACTER PER REGION → PLAIN WHITE FIELD**

The five principal fronts dominate each sheet. The five exact profiles and five direct rears are mandatory subordinate reconstruction views; allocate them compactly without reducing the primary fronts below useful body/material analysis scale.

#### 5.3.74 TRUE FRONT MEANS ZERO UNREQUESTED YAW — HARD / ABSOLUTE

For every principal front:

- face and torso point directly toward the viewer;
- shoulders remain frontal except for genuine anatomical asymmetry;
- hip/lower-torso orientation remains frontal except for genuine anatomical asymmetry;
- arms are relaxed and analytically clear;
- no raised arm, hand-behind-head gesture, contrapposto, hip cock, crossed-leg glamour stance, or 3/4 camera substitution appears;
- no dramatic perspective exaggeration substitutes for body design.

The front must allow direct comparison of the five underlying bodies.

#### 5.3.75 MANDATORY SIDE/REAR VIEWS ARE ANALYTICAL SUPPORT — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Every initial Phase-1 identity has mandatory analytical support views on its sheet: one exact 90-degree profile and one direct rear of the same frozen body. They never become alternate poses, three-quarter illustrations, different anatomy, or character variants. Their scale is subordinate to the dominant front, but they may not be omitted to enlarge the fronts; layout must instead preserve the exact five-identity / fifteen-depiction contract while keeping the dominant fronts analytically useful.

#### 5.3.76 NO EXTRA PRESENTATION MODULES — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Do not use face studies, material boards, palette strips, symbols, concept icons, decorative miniatures, statistics, narrative captions, or page ornament to compensate for weak body design. The available canvas belongs to the five complete characters.

#### 5.3.77 FORMAT MUST SURVIVE RENDERER INTERPRETATION — HARD

The FINAL RENDER CONTEXT explicitly states canvas ratio, five-region sequence, five principal full-body fronts, five exact 90-degree profiles, five direct rears, true-front camera for the dominant views, neutral stance, complete extents, plain white field, and cross-view identity lock. The renderer is never asked to infer this from `turnaround`, `reference sheet`, or `character sheet` alone.

#### 5.3.78 NO PAGE-LEVEL CONTENT MAY COMPETE WITH CHARACTER READABILITY — HARD

No decorative framing, typography, environment, lighting spectacle, or page-level effect may compete with the bodies or carry a supplied concept. Body-bound detached identity morphology remains character-owned and is not page decoration.

#### 5.3.79 RENDERER-FACING SERIALIZATION MUST BE POSITIVE, FEATURE-COMPLETE, AND SPECIFIC — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The renderer-facing package uses positive visual construction language and contains a complete independent block for each character. Every block explicitly includes:

- body regime and dominant silhouette;
- face/head/eyes/hair identity;
- major proportions and mass distribution;
- every mandatory major recognizable characteristic of every primary concept, with exact implementation;
- pairwise and higher-order fusion results as concrete visible structures;
- appendage count, origin, scale, form, material, topology, and body relation where applicable;
- material hierarchy with materially distinct illustrated behaviors;
- body-bound detached identity structures where certified;
- intrinsic body surface architecture across the chest and lower torso, with no separate analytical underlayer;
- exact colour/value hierarchy;
- true-front neutral-standing presentation.

Internal proof language is omitted, but **no mandatory feature is omitted merely because the prompt is already long**. If the compact block cannot retain a mandatory wing, horn, tail, cavity, material system, or higher-order fusion hook, recompile wording rather than deleting the feature.

#### 5.3.80 Complete fusion as a resolved construction problem — HARD

**Rule.** Resolve all primary concepts together into the whole character body before subordinate enrichment and final character-identity finishing. Each initial character must use an independently authored complete construction strategy.

**Explanation.** A body is not a neutral mannequin plus concept layers. Fusion changes relationships: how matter forms joints, how topology changes support, how appendages originate, how proportions respond, how materials meet, how face/head/hair may participate, and how the silhouette is organized. Complete-set influence does not require identical treatment in every region or a separate manifestation for every possible body system.

**Minimum evidence.** Every primary-concept pair must have a genuine visible physical consequence. For n ≥ 3, at least one unmistakable higher-order construction must depend on the complete set. Several pairwise and higher-order obligations may coexist in the same integrated structure. No fixed number of major structures or affected regions is required.

The body as a whole must still be authored under the fusion, and every concept's major recognizable vocabulary must remain present. Additional propagation into other systems is used only when it strengthens recognizability, coherence, interaction evidence, material identity, visual hierarchy, or premium character appeal.

**Readability.** Concrete visual evidence is mandatory. For a concept with major recognizable characteristics, retain that complete major vocabulary while changing how it physically exists. For an abstract concept, record an explicit authored physical interpretation and show its actual consequences. Do not claim universal word-for-word recognition from arbitrary anatomy; do not use that uncertainty as permission for a vague mapping.

**Validation.** For every concept, identify its major recognizable vocabulary, principal carrier(s), required pairwise interaction consequences, complete-set higher-order participation where applicable, and exact positive renderer-description clauses. Then compare complete bodies and causal strategies. Colour-only cues, labels, external presentation, isolated symbols, or redundant saturation cannot supply the pass.

#### 5.3.81 Cross-domain fusion stress test — HARD / OPERATIONAL

For any concept set spanning morphology, material, topology, force/process, or abstract organization, verify that every domain materially changes the same character rather than occupying isolated feature slots.

At least one major or strong-meso structure must depend on the complete set. Each participating concept changes that structure's geometry, topology, material behavior, proportion, support, continuity, or attachment. Within each applicable initial sheet group, the five characters must solve their complete interactions through different causal architectures rather than relocating or recolouring one recipe; the global review additionally rejects severe cross-sheet reuse.

This stress test is category-based and introduces no concept content of its own.
#### 5.3.88 Additional universal construction domains

**Rule.** Unfamiliar concepts use the same decomposition, complete-fusion, body-membership, and comparison process. Extend the vocabulary without weakening the method.

| Domain | Body-level construction opportunities | Invalid shortcut |
|---|---|---|
| Mechanical/artificial | Constituent frames, hinges, linked segments, telescoping anatomy, joint interfaces, internal mechanisms | Wearing machinery over a generic body |
| Botanical | Vascular organization, root-like joints, lamellar masses, petal-derived membranes, controlled branching | Carrying flowers or adding a floral background |
| Architectural | Vault-like intrinsic passages, ribbed support, nested planes, buttress-like body relationships | Buildings or miniature architecture beside the character |
| Celestial/cosmic | Intrinsic layered matter, coherent internal gradients, bounded luminous structures, spatially organized anatomy; a body-bound detached identity structure only when it is genuine major morphology | Floating planets, stars, generic halos used as decoration, or star-field scenery |
| Fluid/state | Coherent soft volumes, membrane boundaries, layered phase transitions, compressible anatomy | Splashes, puddles, droplets, or smoke outside the body |
| Psychological/behavioral/abstract | Explicit authored form relationships such as tension, repetition, interrupted continuity, compression, density, direction | Personality, expression, slogan, or story as sole embodiment |
| Object/tool-derived | Category-relevant geometry transformed into constitutive anatomy and relationships | Holding or wearing the object |

**Application.** A mechanical × botanical combination must make organismal branching and articulation jointly determine the body. One robotic arm plus one flower leg is additive partitioning. An architectural × fluid combination must form anatomy through their interaction, not place a liquid female character inside a building.

**Validation.** Each domain entry is a vocabulary pool. No entry alone proves complete fusion or independence. Resolve exact quantities, dimensions, material boundaries, and relationships in each blueprint before applying the gate.

### 5.4 Complete concept fusion and higher-order embodiment

For every five-character sheet, the compiler must author five different answers to the same question:

> **How does this exact complete concept set become this particular female character?**

The answers must differ at the construction level.

#### 5.4.1 Character 01 must not become the canonical template

Character 01 is not the base character.

Characters 02–05 must not be derived by modifying Character 01.

Character 01 itself must be authored as one independent solution among five.

No ordering priority exists between the five fusion architectures.

#### 5.4.2 Each character requires a unique fusion locus

Across the five characters, deliberately vary where the highest-order concept interaction becomes most structurally visible.

Possible loci include, when appropriate to the concepts:

- cranial architecture;
- facial architecture;
- neck and shoulder transition;
- chest and ribcage topology;
- torso core;
- spine and back;
- hip/lower-torso region and hip architecture;
- arms and hands;
- legs;
- distributed whole-body topology;
- appendage roots;
- internal cavities;
- multiple interacting loci.

No single locus may become the default recipe for all five. The set fails if most characters place the dominant higher-order construction in the same region with only parameter changes. Major loci may repeat only when the governing structure, distribution logic, and cross-concept interactions are otherwise substantially different.
#### 5.4.3 Each character requires unique concept-carrier distribution

For every character, explicitly resolve:

- primary carrier;
- secondary carrier;
- tertiary carrier;
- shared carriers;
- higher-order carrier;
- carrier distribution;
- carrier density;
- carrier scale;
- carrier direction;
- carrier material;
- carrier asymmetry.

The five distributions must materially differ. Distribution includes not only where each concept appears, but also how strongly each concept transforms each anatomical system and how much of the female/human-readable base remains visible. Repeating the same transformation ratio across the set is a convergence failure.
#### 5.4.4 No repeated feature grammar

The following pattern is insufficient:

> **same body + concept A feature + concept B feature + concept C feature**.

It remains insufficient even when:

- colours differ;
- heights differ;
- hair differs;
- appendage counts differ slightly;
- identity finishing differs where applicable;
- markings differ.

The causal construction itself must differ. If the five characters can all be truthfully summarized by one carrier sentence with only parameter substitutions—for example one repeated body grammar plus different heights, colours, or weights—the population fails and the overlapping constructions must be rebuilt.
#### 5.4.5 Independent aesthetic authorship

Each character must be refined as if she were the sole protagonist of an anime/manhwa work.

This means every character receives deliberate decisions for:

- facial identity;
- eye identity;
- hair identity;
- character-identity finishing;
- body proportions;
- body regime;
- build;
- chest architecture;
- shoulder architecture;
- waist architecture;
- hip/lower-torso architecture;
- limb proportions;
- appendages;
- internal topology;
- asymmetry;
- material organization;
- colour organization;
- silhouette;
- micro-detail hierarchy.

No parameter may be varied merely to satisfy a checklist.

Every parameter must contribute to a coherent and aesthetically refined individual design.

#### 5.4.6 UNIVERSAL CONCEPT-FUSION EXAMPLE: THREE CONCEPTS — REQUIRED

For the same arbitrary:

the required result is:

> **CONCEPT A × CONCEPT B × CONCEPT C → COMPLETE FEMALE BODY ARCHITECTURE**

The compiler must resolve the combined concepts before constructing the body.

The concepts jointly determine:

- body regime;
- proportions;
- body substrate;
- internal topology;
- appendage roots;
- appendages;
- face participation;
- hair participation;
- eye participation;
- material architecture;
- asymmetry;
- silhouette;
- concept-interaction structures.

Each concept remains visibly identifiable.

No concept is relegated to an accent.

No concept is transferred to the environment.

No concept is transferred to an independent detached effect; certified body-bound detached identity morphology remains character construction.

The final body must itself reveal the combination.

#### 5.4.7 UNIVERSAL DIFFERENTIATION EXAMPLE — REQUIRED

Within any initial sheet group whose relevant slots are fixed to the same concepts, the five characters are valid only when that shared concept set produces five different ways of physically constructing the character. When selector-resolved primary sets differ, the same independence standard applies to each character's own frozen resolved set and to the group's resulting construction diversity.

For example, the population may differ simultaneously in:

- stature regime;
- body proportion logic;
- torso architecture;
- hip/lower-torso region architecture;
- chest architecture;
- limb architecture;
- skeletal architecture;
- internal topology;
- aperture architecture;
- appendage origins;
- appendage count;
- appendage pairing;
- appendage form;
- material architecture;
- fusion locus;
- pairwise interactions;
- higher-order interactions;
- asymmetry;
- silhouette;
- face architecture;
- character-identity finishing.

The exact differences must be coherent with each character.

They must not be arbitrary.

They must not weaken any supplied concept.

#### 5.4.8 UNIVERSAL ANATOMICAL BODY-CARRIER EXAMPLE — INVALID

For any concept that conventionally suggests an external phenomenon:

> character + surrounding phenomenon

is insufficient.

The phenomenon does not count as body materialization.

A background representation does not count.

A surrounding effect does not count.

A floating symbol does not count.

A detached object does not count.

The character must physically embody the concept.

#### 5.4.9 UNIVERSAL ANATOMICAL BODY-CARRIER EXAMPLE — REQUIRED

For any concept with an external real-world referent:

> **REFERENT PROPERTIES → BODY-LEVEL PHYSICAL CONSEQUENCES**

The exact consequences may occur through:

- body geometry;
- topology;
- substrate;
- internal structure;
- appendage roots;
- appendages;
- proportions;
- face;
- eyes;
- hair;
- material;
- asymmetry;
- silhouette.

The implementation is different for each character.

The concept remains visible.

The environment remains analytically empty.

#### 5.4.10 UNIVERSAL STYLE EXAMPLE — INVALID

For the required 2D drawing grammar, do not serialize:

> generic anime character, high detail, cinematic 3D materials.

> realistic 3D body with anime face.

> physically rendered fantasy character with a 2D filter.

> choose whichever rendering approach looks best.

#### 5.4.11 UNIVERSAL STYLE EXAMPLE — REQUIRED

For the required 2D grammar, serialize the resolved drawing language:

> **2D drawn linework + defined contour hierarchy + defined face/eye grammar + defined hair grammar + defined body shape language + defined shadow language + defined highlight language + defined colour behavior + defined material illustration + defined detail hierarchy**

The exact drawing values are resolved within the embedded envelope in Section 2 and recorded in the generation package.

The style must actively shape the rendering.

#### 5.4.12 UNIVERSAL FORMAT EXAMPLE — INVALID

Do not rely on:

> character sheet.

> turnaround sheet.

> five character lineup.

Those phrases do not specify the actual required geometry.

#### 5.4.13 UNIVERSAL FORMAT EXAMPLE — REQUIRED

Serialize the initial Phase-1 population as:

> **ONE CONTINUOUS 16:9 LANDSCAPE IMAGE**
>
> **FIVE LEFT-TO-RIGHT CHARACTER REGIONS**
>
> **FIVE LARGE UNCROPPED FULL-BODY TRUE-FRONT NEUTRAL-STANDING CHARACTERS**
>
> **PLAIN WHITE FIELD**
>
> **MANDATORY SMALL EXACT PROFILE + DIRECT REAR FOR EVERY IDENTITY, SUBORDINATE TO THE DOMINANT FRONTS WITHOUT CROPPING OR LOSS OF READABILITY**
>
> **NO EXTRA PRESENTATION MODULES**

The executable initial-sheet presentation contract is exactly five dominant true-front bodies plus five exact 90-degree profiles plus five direct rears. Internal body resolution remains three-dimensional, and the mandatory profile/rear views may not be omitted.

### 5.5 Body architecture and proportions

No initial-sheet Clean Renderer-Facing Description may be certified until all fifteen characters exist as complete independent frozen blueprints and the target sheet's five are bound to its revision set.

Each blueprint MUST specify:

1. whole-body architecture;
2. explicit relative scale/height;
3. head/body ratio;
4. torso/leg ratio;
5. shoulder/hip/lower-torso region relationship;
6. ribcage;
7. waist;
8. hip/lower-torsos;
9. chest geometry and projection;
10. limb length/thickness/taper;
11. musculature;
12. skeleton/joints;
13. hands/feet;
14. head/face/eyes;
15. hair architecture;
16. body substrate/material;
17. internal topology;
18. cavities/openings/replacement structures where applicable;
19. surface architecture;
20. appendage count/origin/attachment/form/material;
21. asymmetry;
22. dominant silhouette;
23. direct body carrier for every concept;
24. every pairwise fusion structure;
25. higher-order fusion structures;
26. sub-concept 1 physical consequence;
27. sub-concept 2 physical consequence;
27. female-character identity architecture and character-first proof;
27. distinctive structures;
27. secondary character-identity finishing only after all structural requirements pass.

A semantic adjective is not a blueprint. “Regal,” “chaotic,” “powerful,” “feathery,” “crystalline,” or similar language never substitutes for concrete physical resolution.

The blueprint must remain reconstructable after concept labels are removed.

Height is only one axis of differentiation.

The workflow MUST actively author every major body parameter toward a distinct solution.

Across the five characters deliberately vary combinations of:

- absolute height;
- head-to-body relationship;
- neck length;
- shoulder width;
- shoulder slope;
- torso length;
- torso depth;
- ribcage geometry;
- waist length;
- waist width;
- hip/lower-torso region width;
- hip projection;
- leg length;
- thigh proportion;
- calf proportion;
- arm length;
- forearm proportion;
- hand scale;
- foot scale;
- chest volume;
- chest projection;
- chest placement;
- upper-body mass distribution;
- lower-body mass distribution;
- musculature;
- skeletal impression;
- joint geometry;
- body taper;
- silhouette rhythm;
- structural asymmetry.

The result must not look like five female characters generated from one mannequin.

#### 5.5.1 Petite means compact, not caricaturally compressed

A petite character is allowed and encouraged where useful.

Petite means a deliberate attractive female character design with compact scale and coherent anatomy.

Petite does NOT mean:

- oversized head;
- caricaturally compressed proportions;
- compressed anatomy;
- mascot proportions;
- miniature body copied from the same template;
- simplified anatomy.

A petite character must remain protagonist-grade.

#### 5.5.2 Extreme scale must remain aesthetically controlled

Very short and very tall characters are both permitted.

Neither extreme may be implemented by simply stretching or shrinking the same body.

Scale must propagate through the complete construction:

- head relationship;
- torso proportions;
- hip/lower-torso region;
- limbs;
- appendages;
- silhouette;
- concept-fusion architecture.

#### 5.5.3 Every parameter is an aesthetic decision

The compiler must not treat body parameters as random seeds.

For each character, the parameter set must answer:

> **Why does this exact combination produce a visually compelling independent character?**

The answer must be structural and visual.

Randomness without design purpose is not differentiation.

#### 5.5.4 "PETITE" IS A VALID SCALE REGIME; CARICATURALLY COMPRESSED PROPORTIONS ARE NOT A PETITE SOLUTION — HARD / ABSOLUTE

A small-scale or compact female character is permitted.

A petite character MUST remain a fully resolved attractive female anime/manhwa character with coherent head-to-body relationships, developed facial structure, deliberate proportions, refined anatomy, deliberate silhouette, and complete character-design finish.

Petite MUST NOT be implemented through:

- caricaturally compressed body simplification;
- oversized head relative to the resolved body;
- toy-like body construction;
- mascot compression;
- caricatural limb shortening;
- simplified facial geometry;
- simplified hands or feet;
- abbreviated anatomy;
- miniature treatment of a normally proportioned character;
- reduced detail quality;
- intentionally infantile visual construction.

A petite character is a deliberate body-regime choice, not a quality reduction.

The workflow MUST support the full scale range from extremely tall to very short while preserving the same professional character-quality floor.

Height divergence is therefore compatible with quality parity.

The population may contain a very tall character and a very short character simultaneously.

The short character MUST remain physically and aesthetically deliberate rather than becoming a compressed version of the taller character.

#### 5.5.5 BODY-SYSTEM AUTHORSHIP MUST BE DELIBERATE, NOT RANDOM — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Differentiation is not random parameter mutation.

The compiler MUST NOT independently roll arbitrary values for height, limb lengths, chest architecture, hip/lower-torso region architecture, appendages, colour, apertures, or materials without an integrated character-design rationale grounded entirely in the supplied visual concept set and the resolved fusion architecture.

Each character must instead receive a coherent aesthetic design in which its physical parameters reinforce one another.

For every character, the following must be solved as a connected design:

- stature;
- head/body relationship;
- neck;
- shoulders;
- ribcage;
- torso length and geometry;
- waist geometry;
- hip/lower-torso region geometry;
- hip structure;
- chest volume;
- chest projection;
- chest placement;
- arm length;
- forearm-to-upper-arm relationship;
- hand scale;
- finger construction;
- leg length;
- thigh-to-calf relationship;
- foot scale;
- skeletal structure;
- musculature;
- internal topology;
- body substrate;
- major appendage system;
- face;
- eyes;
- hair;
- colours;
- materials;
- asymmetry;
- silhouette.

The result must read as a deliberately designed whole.

Changing one parameter is insufficient when the rest of the body continues to follow another character's architecture.

The compiler MUST actively modify multiple mutually reinforcing parameters together whenever a character requires a distinct body regime.

A very tall design must not merely be a stretched version of another design.

A very short design must not merely be a compressed version of another design.

A broad-shoulder design must not merely add shoulder width to a reused torso.

A narrow-shoulder design must not merely reduce shoulder width in the same body.

A long-legged design must modify the relationships between hip/lower-torso region, femur, lower leg, torso, and feet coherently.

A compact-legged design must independently resolve those same relationships.

A distinct chest architecture must be accompanied by its own torso, ribcage, shoulder, waist, and silhouette relationships.

A distinct hip/lower-torso architecture must be accompanied by a corresponding lower-body mass distribution and leg architecture.

Every major body regime must therefore be a designed system, not an isolated parameter adjustment.

#### 5.5.6 UNIVERSAL "BODY AS DESIGN SPACE" RULE — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The complete body is the available design space.

No physical system is protected from concept influence merely because it is conventionally associated with unrelated character design.

When the supplied concept fusion can reasonably and visibly alter:

- height;
- head;
- face;
- eyes;
- hair;
- neck;
- shoulders;
- clavicles;
- ribcage;
- chest;
- waist;
- hip/lower-torso region;
- hips;
- abdomen;
- spine;
- arms;
- hands;
- fingers;
- legs;
- feet;
- skeleton;
- musculature;
- internal topology;
- cavities;
- appendages;
- body substrate;
- material transitions;
- asymmetry;
- silhouette;
- colour;
- character-identity finishing, including optional cosmetics where selected;

the compiler must consider that design space.

No parameter is protected merely because it was not previously modified.

No parameter is required to remain conventional.

No parameter may be changed merely for randomness.

The parameter must be changed deliberately as part of an aesthetically coherent character solution.

#### 5.5.7 UNIVERSAL "DESIGNED EXTREMITY" RULE — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Extreme morphology is allowed.

Extreme morphology is encouraged when necessary to distinguish the concept-fusion solutions.

Extreme morphology must still be:

- physically readable;
- structurally connected;
- deliberately proportioned;
- aesthetically coherent;
- character-specific;
- concept-specific;
- rendered clearly.

The compiler must prefer a coherent extreme solution over a tame generic solution when the concept fusion supports it.

The compiler must never reduce an unusual concept to a tame body merely because conventional anatomy is easier to render.

The compiler must never make the unusualness random.

### 5.6 Morphology and appendages

#### 5.6.1 Appendages are structural — HARD

Major appendages are part of body identity, not decoration.

For every character resolve:

- count;
- origin zone;
- attachment depth;
- symmetry/asymmetry;
- spatial distribution;
- length;
- thickness;
- branching;
- form;
- material/substrate;
- jointing/flexion;
- axial integration;
- interaction with topology;
- silhouette contribution.

#### 5.6.2 Population-level appendage divergence — HARD

The five characters must not converge on a repeated appendage template.

First determine whether an appendage class belongs to a concept's **major recognizable vocabulary**, is produced by the complete fusion, or is merely a supporting/optional possibility.

- If the appendage is part of the major recognizable vocabulary, every character retains that appendage class and differentiates its complete physical architecture.
- If the appendage is required by a particular character's fusion but not by the concept vocabulary generally, it remains mandatory for that character.
- If the appendage is only supporting or optional, it may be used in some characters and omitted in others when that improves the complete design.

The same required appendage class may therefore appear across all five characters while varying radically in number, pairing, origin, attachment depth, direction, size, proportion, length, width, thickness, curvature, taper, branching, segmentation, articulation, jointure, symmetry/asymmetry, material, visibly rendered texture, internal construction, support system, relation to neighboring anatomy, topology interaction, concept interaction, and silhouette contribution.

The renderer must never infer a default appendage set from a concept label.

#### 5.6.3 Concept visibility without separation

Appendage diversity does **not** mean rendering each concept as a separate appendage or deleting a required iconic appendage from some characters.

Every major appendage must be evaluated as a possible shared structure in the fusion problem. Its final architecture must emerge from the complete concept construction wherever interaction is physically meaningful.

When appendages are present because the major recognizable vocabulary or resolved fusion requires them, they are authored as major body systems rather than generic attachments. Supporting/optional appendages are added only when they strengthen the character.

Across the five characters vary, as applicable:

- number and number of pairs;
- singular versus paired structures;
- left/right symmetry or asymmetry;
- origin and attachment region;
- attachment depth and height;
- attachment direction;
- size and scale relative to the body;
- length, width, and thickness;
- curvature and taper;
- branching and segmentation;
- articulation and jointure;
- material and surface/texture organization;
- density, overlap, spacing, orientation, and termination shape;
- internal support architecture;
- relation to the silhouette;
- relation to cavities/topology;
- relation to other concept-derived structures.

The phrases “has wings,” “has horns,” or “has a tail” are not architectural specifications. Any appendage actually used must be resolved into a character-specific system.

#### 5.6.4 Appendages must participate in fusion

An appendage must not be a concept-specific sticker.

Its:

- origin;
- material;
- geometry;
- orientation;
- attachment;
- surface;
- asymmetry;

should be influenced by the combined concept system wherever physically meaningful.

#### 5.6.5 Appendage systems must alter the body

The root of a major appendage must be physically coherent with the body.

Where appropriate, the body may change around the attachment through:

- widened structural zones;
- altered surface topology;
- layered transitions;
- reinforced-looking anatomy;
- altered planes;
- cavities;
- ridges;
- folds of intrinsic material;
- other concept-derived structures.

The exact consequence depends on the concept set.

The principle does not.

#### 5.6.6 USEFUL DIFFERENTIATION = DIFFERENT DESIGN LOGIC — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Useful differentiation is the difference between two genuinely different visual solutions to the same unresolved concept-combination problem.

Useful differentiation is present when a viewer could inspect the two bodies after removing:

- colour;
- hairstyle styling;
- optional cosmetic/marking finish;
- markings;
- expression;
- pose;
- lighting;
- rendering treatment;
- page presentation;
- micro-detail;

and still conclude that the two characters were constructed according to different physical design logics.

Useful differentiation is absent when the characters share the same:

- body mass arrangement;
- torso construction;
- proportion logic;
- appendage-root logic;
- topology logic;
- aperture logic;
- substrate logic;
- fusion locus;
- concept-interaction structure;
- silhouette logic;
- asymmetry logic.

even if superficial features differ.

The compiler MUST therefore test not only whether the bodies are technically different, but whether the difference is useful.

The validator must distinguish:

> **PARAMETER DIFFERENCE**

from:

> **CONSTRUCTION DIFFERENCE**

and must accept only the latter as the primary population-differentiation mechanism.

Parameter changes remain permitted and useful only after independent construction differences already exist.

#### 5.6.7 NO RELATED-CHARACTER FAMILY EFFECT — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The five characters MUST NOT visually read as siblings, alternate versions, successive forms, same-character derivatives, uniform species variants, or one visual template with different settings.

Shared concept identity is mandatory.

Shared character identity is not.

A repeated face family is a failure.

A repeated body family is a failure.

A repeated silhouette family is a failure.

A repeated appendage family is a failure.

A repeated fusion family is a failure.

A repeated material family is a failure.

A repeated colour recipe is a failure when it makes the five characters visually converge.

A common art style is allowed and required.

A common concept set is required.

A common quality floor is required.

The physical character identities themselves must nevertheless be independently authored.

The concept set is the common design problem.

The character solution is independent.

#### 5.6.8 APPENDAGE ORIGIN IS A DESIGN AXIS — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Appendages MUST NOT repeatedly emerge from the most obvious or convenient body location.

The compiler must actively search different origin systems across the population.

Possible origin families include:

- upper axial origin;
- scapular/dorsal origin;
- thoracic origin;
- rib-derived origin;
