- cervical origin;
- cranial origin;
- hip/lower-torso origin;
- hip-origin structures;
- limb-root origin;
- spinal origin;
- distributed whole-body emergence;
- integrated body-plane emergence;
- internal-cavity emergence.

These are possibilities, not a fixed menu.

The actual chosen origin must be compatible with the complete fusion and must be aesthetically refined.

#### 5.6.9 APERTURE / CAVITY / OPENING SYSTEMS MUST NOT CONVERGE — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Any concept capable of producing openings, cavities, absence, discontinuity, perforation, penetration, rupture, voids, channels, or related structural conditions must be explored through body topology.

The compiler must vary across characters:

- body region;
- count;
- pair count;
- single versus multiple;
- symmetry;
- asymmetry;
- scale;
- depth;
- through-opening versus recessed structure;
- nested structure;
- branching;
- orientation;
- axial relation;
- load-bearing relation;
- attachment relation;
- relation to appendages;
- relation to substrate;
- relation to other concepts.

An aperture repeatedly placed on the same leg, arm, abdomen, or other convenient region across the entire population is a population-convergence failure unless the complete concept fusion independently and necessarily produces the same locus.

The aperture system must visibly belong to the body.

The aperture system must materially influence the body's construction.

The aperture system must not be represented by detached portals, floating holes, environmental windows, or surrounding effects.

### 5.7 Apertures, topology, and asymmetry

When a supplied concept supports openings, breaches, voids, cavities, perforations, channels, fractures, or altered internal topology, these structures MUST be treated as body architecture.

They must not repeatedly appear in one convenient anatomical location.

Across the five characters vary:

- location;
- count;
- scale;
- depth;
- orientation;
- branching;
- edge geometry;
- distribution;
- asymmetry;
- relation to skeletal or surface topology;
- material around the opening;
- interior visual treatment;
- transition into surrounding anatomy.

The same fixed complete concept set may therefore produce radically different aperture architectures while retaining concept recognition; selector-resolved candidates apply the same construction principle to their own frozen complete sets.

An aperture must be a meaningful structural consequence, not a decorative mark.

### 5.8 Face, eyes, hair, and character-identity finishing

The face/head system is a major identity system and must be authored with the same seriousness as the body. Character identity is mandatory; conventional cosmetics are optional.

For every character independently resolve, as applicable:

- skull/head shape;
- face length and width;
- cranial proportion;
- forehead proportion;
- brow position, geometry, and thickness;
- eye size, width, height, spacing, angle, and depth;
- upper and lower eyelid construction;
- iris size, architecture, colour organization, and internal pattern;
- pupil construction;
- sclera treatment;
- eye highlights;
- lash design;
- nose length/form and stylized construction;
- mouth width and geometry;
- lip structure;
- philtrum treatment where stylistically applicable;
- cheek volume and planes;
- jaw geometry;
- chin geometry;
- ear architecture;
- facial asymmetry where useful;
- facial material transitions;
- hairline and face framing;
- relationship among all facial features.

The five characters must remain recognizably different when hair colour and conventional makeup are mentally removed. Their cranial and facial construction itself must distinguish them.

#### 5.8.1 Character identity is broader than cosmetics — HARD / ABSOLUTE

A character with no makeup whatsoever can have extremely strong identity. A character with elaborate makeup can still fail if her underlying face is a generic copy.

Identity may be reinforced through deliberately selected systems such as:

- pigmentation;
- scars;
- intrinsic markings;
- unusual sclera or iris treatment;
- lashes;
- eyeliner;
- eyeshadow;
- brow styling;
- blush;
- lip colour;
- skin-value treatment;
- nail treatment where visible;
- facial material transitions;
- controlled identity-bearing highlights;
- other character-specific finishing details.

No individual item in this list is universally mandatory. What is mandatory is a fully authored, memorable identity. Every initial Frozen Character Specification must include at least two character-specific identity-finishing decisions beyond simple hair-colour or body-colour substitution. These may come from the existing identity systems above and must reinforce the character's already-distinct facial, ocular, cranial, hair, hand/claw, pigmentation, marking, material-transition, or asymmetry construction rather than functioning as detachable decoration.
#### 5.8.2 Identity finishing cannot replace structure — HARD

The following remains invalid:

> **same face + different makeup/markings = five characters**.

The face architecture itself must differ. Identity finishing then completes or reinforces that difference.

**Character identity is optimized for memorability, not detail count.** A comparatively simple face or head can be exceptionally strong when its geometry, feature relationships, eyes, framing hair, material treatment, or one deliberate identity hook make it unmistakable. Conversely, a face can contain many eyelashes, markings, highlights, colours, and micro-details while remaining generic. The workflow must not add facial or hair complexity merely to prove that identity was developed; every identity-bearing detail must improve recognition, appeal, or coherence.

#### 5.8.3 CONSTRUCTION QUALITY DOES NOT MEAN "MORE DETAIL" — HARD

Construction quality is not achieved by indiscriminately increasing detail.

The compiler must satisfy these structural priorities:

- deliberate silhouette;
- deliberate anatomy;
- controlled major forms;
- developed face/head identity;
- developed eyes;
- coherent hair;
- refined material hierarchy;
- deliberate colour organization;
- intentional asymmetry where useful;
- readable appendages and topology where present;
- controlled linework;
- 2D-native shading;
- micro-detail subordinated to the major design.

A noisy image is not equivalent to a high-quality image. A highly detailed generic body is not equivalent to a high-quality character. A complex concept treatment that obscures the character is not equivalent to a high-quality concept fusion.

#### 5.8.4 UNIVERSAL CONCEPT-FUSION EXAMPLE: THREE CONCEPTS — INVALID

For any arbitrary three-concept combination, the following is insufficient:

> generic female body + Concept A feature + Concept B feature + Concept C feature.

It is also insufficient to slightly alter proportions between five copies of that structure, move one feature from one limb to another while retaining the same construction logic, or change only colour, appendage count, aperture placement, hair, markings, or makeup.

Those changes are not enough when the underlying fusion architecture is repeated.

#### 5.8.5 Character-identity completion after structural certification — HARD / ABSOLUTE

**Rule.** Every body receives deliberately authored face, eye, hair, scar/marking where selected, pigmentation/material identity, and other applicable finishing after structural independence has passed. Character-identity completion is mandatory; conventional cosmetics are optional.

**Explanation.** “After” describes pre-render design order. Do not render a bare structural image and then edit it to add identity. The single authorized render already uses the complete certified character description. Primary anatomical head, eye sockets, cranial form, and indispensable structural hair/matter systems must already be resolved sufficiently for the structural blueprint; later finishing develops recognizability without pretending it created body independence.

**General example.** Two bodies that differ only through long versus short hair or one scar cannot pass the structural gate. Two already distinct bodies still need independently authored identity finishing.

**Application.** One body may have a broad angular face, narrow inset eyes of different colours, and rigid lamellar hair rooted along an asymmetric scalp. Another may have a short rounded cranial shape, large dark irises, and a compact mass of coherent flame-like hair. Resolve both through their own complete concept combination and subordinate choices rather than assigning one generic face and changing makeup afterward.

**Failure.** Copying one face five times, giving every character the same hair architecture, using one unusual eye as the only trace of an abstract concept, or using finishing to hide body convergence fails.

**Validation.** Confirm the earlier structural certificate, exact identity specification, independent recognizability, concept accordance, material membership, and consistency across views. Structural changes introduced during finishing invalidate the affected earlier certificate until rechecked.

#### 5.8.6 Hair construction and colour organization — HARD

**Rule.** Hair architecture, constituent matter, colour count, placement, and internal organization are unrestricted design spaces within body membership and macro readability. Diversity in actual architecture is mandatory across the population; no fixed hairstyle, material, or hue-count pattern is universal.

**Available parameters.** Resolve root zones, scalp relationship, hairline, parting, fringe, length, volume, mass distribution, grouping, strand width, curvature, taper, branching, segmentation, braiding or other intrinsic organization, rigidity, movement implied by fixed form, asymmetry, termination, substrate, transparency, luminosity, colour transitions, and relationship to eyes, face, horns/ears where present, and body matter.

**Colour organization.** Monocolour hair, separated strand colours, roots-to-tip transitions, multiple colour groups, irregular but designed patches, ordered spectral bands, rainbow-like sequences, and different interior/exterior hair colours are eligible. Count alone does not establish a new identity; distribution and construction matter.

**Material organization.** Hair may consist of conventional fibers, feather-like matter, liquid-like coherent volumes, glass-like strands, metal-like ribbons, fire-like anatomy, or ethereal matter. The selected material must be the hair itself. It must have fixed roots, bounded organized masses, and consistent geometry. Detached sparks, smoke, flame trails, floating strands, or an aura are prohibited in Phase 1.

**Validation.** Compare hair among all characters only after structural independence has already passed. Confirm variation in several relevant dimensions, not five recolours of one hair silhouette. Check front-view roots, internally resolved side/rear continuity, length, grouping, and material behavior.

#### 5.8.7 Eye identity, heterochromia, markings, and scars

**Rule.** Eye and surface identity must be concretely authored and consistent with the fused body. No narrative explanation is required or allowed as an invented substitute.

**Parameters.** Resolve eyelid shape, eye size and spacing, iris diameter and architecture, pupil geometry, sclera material/colour, left/right differences, gradients, internal rings or segmentation, lashes, brows, intrinsic luminous accents, scar locus/shape/depth, marking boundaries, density, distribution, and material relationship.

**Application.** Heterochromia may use two different iris colours with shared drawn construction or distinct iris structures when the blueprint supports it. A scar may interrupt a structural rim and continue as a deliberate material transition. A marking can follow an existing anatomical channel without becoming the sole carrier of that concept.

**Failure.** Automatic identical eyes, arbitrary mismatched eyes unsupported by the design, scars accompanied by invented injury stories, and markings substituted for whole-body fusion fail.

**Validation.** Confirm distinctive facial identity at sheet scale, accurate side assignment, colour consistency, and finishing quality equal to the rest of the body. Identity-finishing suppression for structural comparison means ignoring makeup/marking/colour-only evidence, not erasing necessary anatomy, hair mass, or constituent material.

### 5.9 Materials and colour architecture

The five characters must not converge on one shared colour recipe.

Colour architecture must be independently authored both within each character and across the population.

Every initial character must contain substantial internal body-colour differentiation rather than one hue family spread over nearly all body matter. Resolve at least two clearly distinct major body colour/value regions or an equivalently strong multizone/gradient construction, with the differentiation carried by actual body substrate, concept-derived matter, appendages, intrinsic surfaces, or material transitions rather than only hair, eyes, tiny accents, or lighting. Bichromatic, trichromatic, tetracolour, multicolour, strongly graded, and other coherent systems are all valid.

The five characters must also use materially different colour organization: changing only the dominant hue while preserving the same light/dark placement, accent path, hair/body relationship, and appendage/body relationship is insufficient.

No fixed hue count beyond this internal differentiation floor is mandatory. Architectural differentiation itself is mandatory; numeric hue-count variation is not.

Material differentiation likewise does **not** require every character to contain many different materials. A character may be dominated by one unusual constituent material, or even use one principal material across most of the body, when that is the strongest and most coherent design solution. Material count is not a diversity quota. When multiple materially distinct substances do exist, however, their boundaries and illustrated behavior must remain unmistakably different through their own surface organization, shadow/highlight treatment, edges, granularity, opacity/translucency, internal pattern, rigidity/flow, and other applicable 2D material signals. A deliberately unified single-material character is valid; accidental homogenization of materials that are supposed to be different is not.
#### 5.9.1 Colour architecture must be deliberate

For each character determine, where useful:

- number of major hue families without treating that count as a population quota;
- dominant hue;
- secondary hue;
- accent hues;
- value range;
- saturation range;
- warm/cool balance;
- local contrast;
- material-specific colour;
- distribution over the silhouette.

The palette must reinforce the character's physical construction and focal hierarchy. Several characters may use the same approximate hue count if their value, saturation, temperature, material relationship, placement, and visual hierarchy are genuinely different.

#### 5.9.2 Monochrome is not the default Phase-1 solution

An effectively monochromatic initial character is not valid unless the user explicitly requests monochrome for that character. Similar neighbouring hues may still be used, but the body must retain clearly differentiated major regions through hue, value, temperature, saturation, material-colour relationship, or a strong continuous gradient that remains visible at full-body scale.
#### 5.9.3 Rainbow is a permitted individual solution

A highly varied colour system may be used for one character where it produces a coherent aesthetic.

Rainbow coloration is not a mandatory population target.

The rule is deliberate colour authorship, not forced diversity for its own sake.

### 5.10 Sub-concepts

Every initial Broad Phase-1 character receives exactly **two subordinate sub-concepts** only after that character's complete frozen primary construction is already structurally valid and the required primary differentiation stage has passed. Primary wildcard/selector randomness and Sub-concept-2 randomness are separate systems and must never share pools or eligibility logic.

The hierarchy is absolute:

> **RESOLVED PRIMARY CONCEPT SET = complete, co-dominant, mandatory construction problem for that character**
>
> **SUB-CONCEPT 1 = assistant-selected actual Era / Culture / Civilization influence**
>
> **SUB-CONCEPT 2 = genuinely randomly selected additional concept from a freshly constructed broad/open-ended temporary reservoir, not restricted to canonical SC leaves**
>
> **CHARACTER IDENTITY AUTHORSHIP = proportions, face, hair, materials, colour, structures, and finishing integrated around the complete design**

Sub-concepts intensify an already successful character. They never repair incomplete primary concept materialization and never become equal in authority to the Resolved Primary Concept Set.

#### 5.10.1 Sub-concept 1 — ACTUAL ERA / CULTURE / CIVILIZATION — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Sub-concept 1 supplies one **real, historically or culturally grounded Era / Culture / Civilization source**. It is a world-source vocabulary, not an art movement, design style, genre, franchise, mood, aesthetic label, or invented setting.

Eligible examples include, among many others:

- Ancient Egypt;
- Ptolemaic Egypt;
- Achaemenid Persia;
- Sasanian Persia;
- Classical Greece;
- Hellenistic Greece;
- Roman Republic;
- Roman Empire;
- Byzantine Empire;
- Heian-period Japan;
- Kamakura-period Japan;
- Muromachi-period Japan;
- Edo-period Japan;
- Tang China;
- Song China;
- Ming China;
- Qing China;
- Mughal India;
- Gupta India;
- Khmer Empire;
- Srivijaya;
- Majapahit;
- Aztec civilization;
- Maya civilization;
- Inca civilization;
- Ottoman civilization;
- Abbasid-era culture;
- Andalusi culture;
- medieval France;
- medieval England;
- Renaissance-era Italy as a historical/cultural period rather than “Renaissance style” as an aesthetic label;
- Victorian Britain;
- Belle Époque France as an actual historical-cultural period;
- another real era, culture, or civilization with enough concrete visual knowledge to support non-stereotyped design authorship.

**Forbidden as Sub-concept 1 identities:** Art Deco, Art Nouveau, Baroque-as-style, Rococo-as-style, Gothic-as-style, Brutalism, Bauhaus, cyberpunk, steampunk, dark fantasy, science fiction, post-apocalyptic, “fantasy,” “modern,” “futuristic,” generic mythology labels, genre labels, aesthetic movements, fashion movements, design schools, invented cultures, invented civilizations, invented visual worlds, and any equivalent style-only shorthand.

A historical period that contains an art movement is still eligible **only under the real period/culture/civilization identity**. For example, `Belle Époque France` may be selected; `Art Nouveau` may not. The executor derives a broad grounded visual vocabulary from the historical-cultural source rather than collapsing the source to one art movement.

A mythology may inform the vocabulary only when it is embedded inside the selected real culture/civilization and does not replace that source as the Sub-concept-1 identity. For example, an Ancient Egyptian source may draw on historically associated religious/mythic visual systems without changing the recorded Sub-concept 1 to “Egyptian mythology.”

This sub-concept does not assign biography, ethnicity, nationality, ancestry, religion, historical origin, occupation, social rank, or narrative to the character. It supplies visual relationships that may influence geometry, structural rhythm, ornament logic, material relationships, body patterning, appendage construction, silhouette, hair architecture, facial identity, and later clothing/weapon construction.

It must never be reduced to one stereotypical token. “Tang China” cannot mean one symbol; “Ancient Egypt” cannot mean one headdress; “Roman Empire” cannot mean one laurel; “Edo Japan” cannot mean one generic kimono pattern. The source must be translated through several coherent visual relationships while remaining subordinate to the already-complete primary concept fusion.

The assistant selects Sub-concept 1 through a **reasoned character-specific design choice**, considering fit, population differentiation, novelty, and later clothing/weapon potential. Do not call this selection random unless an explicit user command changes its method.

**Character-specific selection and collision review.** Select Sub-concept 1 independently for each character. There is no per-sheet or fifteen-character uniqueness quota. The same real Era / Culture / Civilization source may recur when it is genuinely the strongest character-specific choice and its physical implementation does not create a first-read, identity, or construction collision. Do not reuse a source merely because it is familiar, visually convenient, or easy for the renderer. If a repeat contributes to actual convergence, repair the causally responsible selection or physical implementation rather than enforcing uniqueness mechanically.

**Validation.** Before certification, record the real source identity and verify:
- it is an actual era, culture, or civilization;
- it is not an art movement, genre, aesthetic style, or invented world;
- any repeated source was independently selected and does not create an unresolved first-read, identity, or construction collision;
- its concrete influence is subordinate to the primary concept fusion;
- its use does not assign the character a biography.

#### 5.10.2 Sub-concept 2 — GENUINELY RANDOM ADDITIONAL CONCEPT

The second sub-concept is effectively one additional concept that the user did not supply. It is selected through an actual random process from a recorded open-ended temporary eligible concept reservoir. That reservoir has no fixed master list, no fixed numerical ceiling, and no preferred canonical rotation.

The temporary reservoir may draw from any concept class that can be materially interpreted by this workflow, including material, process, force, topology, morphology, organization, spatial, temporal, behavioral, aesthetic, ecological, mechanical, or abstract classes. These are classes rather than a fixed concept catalogue. The reservoir must remain open-ended, freshly constructed for the active population, and free from a preferred canonical rotation.

The second sub-concept follows the same concrete-materialization discipline as a supplied concept: it must create a visible physical consequence rather than remain a word, symbol, colour association, accessory, effect, or narrative reference. Its **hierarchical weight remains deliberately subordinate**. It enriches the answer; it does not redefine the question or create an equal additional primary concept.

#### 5.10.3 Genuine-random selection procedure — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Before the draw, establish and record an open-ended temporary eligible reservoir. It must be genuinely broad and heterogeneous rather than a tiny recurring pool. Do not collapse selection into a habitual 20-, 30-, or 50-item rotation, and do not impose any fixed numerical ceiling on how many concepts may be eligible at once. A candidate may be excluded only for a legitimate reason, including:

- explicit task incompatibility;
- inability to operate meaningfully in the active phase;
- near-duplication of a supplied primary concept that adds effectively nothing;
- over-repetition across the current population;
- a high risk of making two complete constructions converge;
- or another standing workflow rule that makes it inapplicable;
- semantic category, strength of conventional associations, or anticipated renderer ambiguity are NOT legitimate exclusion reasons by themselves. The workflow does not pre-exclude otherwise valid concept identities by semantic category; selection eligibility is determined by phase applicability, meaningful distinctness, population diversity, and the standing design rules.

Once the pool is established, perform and record a genuine randomized draw. The assistant MUST NOT privately choose the easiest or most aesthetically convenient concept and describe that choice as random. The selected eligible result is accepted rather than repeatedly rerolled for convenience. Model free-choice, intuition, “I picked one at random,” or an unrecorded selection does **not** count.

If no genuine randomized draw has actually been performed and recorded, the draw is **UNRESOLVED** and cannot be certified. Use a genuine randomized selection or an explicitly user-authorized alternative method before certification.

**Compact internal audit.** The frozen design record must include one concise random-draw audit line for Sub-concept 2 stating the actual mechanism used and the selected outcome for each character. The full temporary reservoir remains internal unless the user requests it. Random-selection bookkeeping is omitted from the Clean Renderer-Facing Description because it carries no visual information.

Randomness determines **which subordinate concept is received**. Authorship determines **how intelligently it is materialized**. The workflow must never pretend that a narrow recurring pool was “effectively infinite”; the temporary reservoir itself must be intentionally expanded and freshly constructed for the current population.

#### 5.10.4 Assignment timing — primary construction comes first

Both sub-concepts are applied only after:

> **COMPLETE PRIMARY CONCEPT MATERIALIZATION → INDEPENDENT CHARACTER CONSTRUCTION → PRIMARY POPULATION DIFFERENTIATION**

have already passed structurally.

Then:

> **SUB-CONCEPT 1 SELECTION → RANDOM SUB-CONCEPT 2 DRAW → SUBORDINATE INTEGRATION → RECHECK AFFECTED STRUCTURE → CHARACTER-IDENTITY / AESTHETIC COMPLETION**

If the Primary Concept Set is not visibly and materially complete before sub-concepts are applied, adding stronger subordinate influences does not solve the failure.

#### 5.10.5 Both sub-concepts must physically affect the character

Neither sub-concept may exist only as metadata. Both need visible consequences, but they do not need the whole-body intensity or complete pairwise burden imposed on the Primary Concept Set.

Their influence should be concentrated where it creates the strongest character identity without obscuring the primary fusion. They should preferably **enter existing primary structures** rather than occupy separate territories. For example, a randomly selected material/state concept may modify an already-resolved aperture, horn, hair mass, membrane, internal channel, or body substrate rather than claiming an isolated body region of its own.

Sub-concepts may interact with primary structures, but they do not require exhaustive pairwise interaction with every primary concept. The Resolved Primary Concept Set remains the mandatory peer fusion; sub-concepts remain subordinate modifiers.

#### 5.10.6 Population-level sub-concept diversity

Across the initial population, avoid repeated complete sub-concept pairs unless a repeated pair is genuinely the strongest design decision or an explicit user constraint requires it.

There is **no fixed numerical reuse cap** for either sub-concept. Reuse is permissible when the resulting physical implementation is genuinely different and does not make the population converge. The workflow must not invent an arbitrary “at most three uses” or similar quota.

The same word may create radically different results because it encounters different independently constructed primary bodies. The same actual Era/Culture/Civilization source is not a preset. The same randomly selected subordinate concept is not a preset.

For Sub-concept 2, the eligible pool is constructed **before** the random draw. Population repetition and convergence concerns are legitimate eligibility filters before selection. Once a genuinely eligible concept has been drawn, do not reroll merely because another concept would be easier or more aesthetically convenient. If a later audit discovers that the drawn concept was actually ineligible under a standing rule that should have excluded it from the pool, record the selection error and rebuild the eligible pool rather than pretending the original draw was valid.

Before certification, record:

- Sub-concept 1 identity and reasoned selection rationale;
- Sub-concept 2 eligible pool and actual random-selection method;
- selected Sub-concept 2 outcome;
- any reuse and the reason it does not cause convergence;
- exact Phase-1 physical consequence of both;
- exact interaction with existing primary structures;
- proof that neither became co-dominant;
- planned stronger Phase-2 clothing influence where applicable;
- planned stronger Phase-3 weapon influence where applicable.

An unresolved or falsely claimed random draw blocks generation.
### 5.11 Within-sheet five-character differentiation, independent authorship, and global fifteen-character collision review

All checkpoint-strength five-character differentiation rules in this section apply **independently to each initial sheet group**: Characters 01–05, Characters 06–10, and Characters 11–15. Every five-way matrix and all ten pairwise comparisons are run three times, once per group. After those proofs pass, perform the additional global fifteen-character collision review in §5.11.GLOBAL. No statement in this section converts the three groups into separate CREATE operations.

#### 5.11.1 No canonical first solution — HARD

A concept combination has **no canonical body solution**.

Character 1 is not the answer to the concept combination. It is merely the first independent physical solution.

Character 1 MUST NOT become:

- a hidden mannequin;
- a reference body;
- a preferred morphology;
- a solution template;
- a baseline for later characters.

Within each sheet, Characters 2–5 are independently solved from their own frozen complete Resolved Primary Concept Sets; fixed slots remain common while autonomous slots may differ.

#### 5.11.2 Same concepts, different bodies — HARD

Every character receives the **same complete Primary Concept Set** with equal conceptual necessity and unmistakable material presence. Visual intensity may be distributed differently when hierarchy remains strong.

The physical materialization must nevertheless differ substantially between characters.

Variation is achieved by changing the physical answer, not by weakening a concept.

For recurring concepts, vary as applicable:

- body region;
- structural carrier;
- proportion consequence;
- topology;
- material/substrate;
- appendage origin;
- appendage count;
- appendage geometry;
- attachment depth;
- length/thickness;
- branching;
- asymmetry;
- internal architecture;
- silhouette contribution;
- interaction mechanism;
- relative spatial organization.

#### 5.11.3 Independent identity lock — HARD

Each character must be conceived as a **new physical identity**, not as a modification of another character.

For every character, deliberately alter multiple major structural systems simultaneously. The solution must diverge materially across applicable systems including stature/scale regime, head-to-body ratio, torso architecture, ribcage depth/shape, shoulder topology, chest architecture and projection, waist construction, hip/lower-torso geometry, limb ratios, joint placement, musculature distribution, skeletal topology, internal topology, body substrate, appendage architecture, asymmetry pattern, and primary silhouette.

Changing only colour, hair, markings, one appendage set, or surface treatment is never sufficient.

A repeated foundational female chassis fails even when colours, faces, wing patterns, horn silhouettes, tails, or surface materials differ. Do not reuse one underlying torso/waist/hip/chest/leg recipe across the group and then restyle it. Differences must be structural in the body itself.

**BODY-CORE CHASSIS FINGERPRINT — HARD / ABSOLUTE.** Before hair styling, colour, markings, surface micro-detail, appendage styling, and presentation evidence are considered, record a body-core fingerprint for each character from: shoulder/ribcage architecture; torso length/depth/curvature; chest architecture and placement; waist length/width; hip/lower-torso geometry and centre of mass; arm/leg length-thickness-taper and joint placement; musculature/skeletal prominence; stature/head-to-body relationship. For every within-sheet pair, the fingerprints must differ materially in multiple connected systems, including **at least three** of the six central chassis systems (shoulder/ribcage, torso, chest, waist, hip/lower-torso, limb architecture) and at least one additional body-regime system (musculature/skeleton, stature/head relationship, or centre-of-mass/mass distribution). A difference that exists only because of wings, horns, tails, hair, palette, markings, surface pattern, or material microtexture does not count. If two characters could plausibly share one underlying body mesh/chassis after removing those secondary systems, the pair fails and one body must be rebuilt before certification.

#### 5.11.3A FICTIONAL PHYSIOGNOMIC AND BODY-FAMILY DIVERGENCE — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The five characters must not cluster around one recurring face/body archetype. Diversity is authored inside fictional character-design space rather than by importing real-world population labels.

Deliberately vary, where compatible with the concept fusion:

- cranial length/width/depth;
- jaw and chin geometry;
- cheek structure;
- eye size, spacing, tilt, lid construction, and iris relationship;
- brow architecture;
- nose bridge/tip/width relationships;
- mouth width, lip geometry, and philtrum/chin spacing;
- ear morphology;
- neck proportion;
- skin or base-substrate hue/value and material behavior;
- hair root pattern, mass distribution, density, length, and silhouette;
- the relationship between face/head scale and the complete body regime.

These are fictional physiognomic design choices, not real-world identity assignments. The goal is five visibly different fictional identities, not five near-identical faces recoloured or restyled.

#### 5.11.4 No repeated-image population — HARD

The five characters MUST NOT reduce to the same character after surface styling is removed.

The following is an explicit population failure:

> **CHARACTER 1 = CHARACTER 2 = CHARACTER 3 = CHARACTER 4 = CHARACTER 5 WITH DIFFERENT COLOUR / SURFACE / APPENDAGE DECORATION**

If any planned initial sheet group reads as five copies of one underlying character, all affected constructions must be revised before rendering. Changing only the weakest member is insufficient when the common root solution caused group-wide convergence. If this collapse occurs only in the actual image, record the failed outcome without rebuilding or retrying it; the global fifteen-character review separately catches cross-sheet reuse.

#### 5.11.5 Material and colour differentiation — HARD

Material and colour architecture are active differentiation systems. The five characters MUST NOT collapse into one population-wide colour/material implementation.

Vary substantially across the population:

- dominant body substrate/material;
- light-to-dark balance;
- major colour distribution;
- value/saturation structure;
- placement of iridescence;
- placement of translucent material;
- contrast between major structural masses;
- material boundaries and transitions.

A shared concept does not require a shared colourway. Colour reinforces identity but never substitutes for structural concept embodiment.

#### 5.11.6 No degraded body allocation — HARD

Design allocation MUST NOT assign an accidentally swollen, crude, caricaturally compressed, amorphous, or otherwise degraded body merely to create numerical variation. Deliberately broad, muscular, powerful, soft, heavy-framed, or full-figured bodies are valid when fully authored. Extreme variation must come from coherent proportion, skeletal architecture, mass distribution, topology, appendage architecture, materialization, face/head identity, and silhouette rather than a lazy mass shortcut.

#### 5.11.7 Equal quality resolution — HARD

All five characters must independently receive main-character-grade resolution. No character may be a simplified placeholder, low-detail filler, generic mannequin, accidental mass-based fallback, caricaturally compressed fallback, or lower-quality version of another character.

#### 5.11.8 No sequential derivation — HARD

Never construct Character N by copying Character N−1 and modifying it.

Never reuse the same concept-fusion recipe with cosmetic changes.

Never use Character 1 as the physical answer that later characters reinterpret.

Each character undergoes an independent body-solution search before comparison.

> **SAME CONCEPT SET → INDEPENDENT SOLUTION SEARCH → FUSION RESOLUTION → BODY RESOLUTION → POPULATION COMPARISON → COLLISION REBUILD**

#### 5.11.9 No shared mannequin — HARD

There is no default female anime body.

Each character must independently resolve:

- height/scale;
- head/body ratio;
- torso/leg ratio;
- shoulder/hip/lower-torso region relationship;
- ribcage volume and geometry;
- waist architecture;
- hip/lower-torso geometry;
- chest volume, projection and placement;
- limb length, thickness and taper;
- musculature;
- skeleton/joints;
- mass distribution;
- head/face/eye geometry;
- internal topology;
- appendage architecture;
- material/substrate;
- asymmetry;
- silhouette;
- concept-fusion architecture.

#### 5.11.10 Perceptually distinct body-range law — HARD

The valid design space has no mandatory “normal-body” template and does not use arbitrary preset height slots. Each initial five-character sheet group MUST contain a visibly meaningful stature range: one character is clearly the shortest, one is clearly the tallest, and the remaining three do not all collapse to one common intermediate stature. Stature differences must be produced by coherent proportion systems rather than simple uniform scaling.

The five characters must have perceptually different body architectures. Their proportion regimes must remain distinguishable in plain silhouette before hair colour, material texture, markings, and concept-specific surface detail are considered.

Variation may use, in any useful combination:

- stature and overall scale;
- head-to-body relationship;
- neck length and thickness;
- shoulder width and slope;
- ribcage length, width, and depth;
- chest architecture;
- waist length and width;
- hip/lower-torso region height/width and hip projection;
- torso-to-leg relationship;
- thigh-to-calf relationship;
- arm proportions;
- hand and foot scale;
- musculature and skeletal prominence;
- centre of mass and mass distribution;
- appendage scale and placement where applicable.

Very short, very tall, elongated, compact, broad, muscular, soft, curvy, full-figured, powerful, or other extreme regimes are fully permitted when they create the stronger character. When strength or muscularity is selected, prefer a visibly athletic, defined construction with deliberate muscle groups and proportion control rather than using extreme bulk or body mass as an easy diversity substitute. A substantially high-mass regime remains valid when the complete character design genuinely benefits from it; it is not a default population slot.

A tall character is not a standard body stretched vertically. A petite character is not the same body reduced in scale. A broad character is not a standard torso with wider shoulders. The selected proportion logic must propagate coherently through the whole body.

The goal is **deliberately authored perceptual diversity with protagonist-grade aesthetics**, not arbitrary extremity.
#### 5.11.11 Full-body differentiation — HARD

Across the five characters, deliberately vary multiple major systems, including:

- height;
- head/body ratio;
- torso/leg ratio;
- chest projection;
- ribcage width/depth;
- shoulders;
- waist;
- hip/lower-torso region;
- hips;
- torso curvature;
- limb length;
- limb thickness;
- hand/foot scale;
- musculature;
- skeletal prominence;
- joint placement;
- mass distribution;
- centre of mass;
- internal topology;
- appendage systems;
- material/substrate;
- asymmetry;
- silhouette.

Do not use accidental/excessive bulk, caricaturally compressed proportions, mascot morphology, or amorphous mass as a shortcut for differentiation. Any extreme body architecture must arise from the actual resolved physical design while remaining intentional, aesthetically successful, and anatomically legible in the chosen stylized anatomy. Deliberate broad or high-mass regimes remain eligible.

#### 5.11.12 Five distinct construction strategies — HARD

Each character must use a different dominant physical construction strategy.

The five strategies must be intentionally orthogonal rather than five mild variations along one axis. Before rendering, record for each character a dominant architecture selected from genuinely different structural answers. The five answers must be materially distinct and must not become a repeated template.

No two characters may share the same dominant architecture merely because their surface materials or appendages differ.

Across a five-character initial sheet, appendage systems must also avoid silent convergence on one hidden default. Wings, horns, tails, fins, crests, membranes, feather masses, branch systems, or equivalent body-bound identity structures should vary where compatible with the frozen concept sets in count, root/origin zone, support logic, proportion, form language, membrane/plate/feather/fiber treatment, symmetry or asymmetry, and silhouette role. Swapping patterning on one repeated wing plan is insufficient.

**APPENDAGE-ARCHITECTURE DIVERGENCE FLOOR — HARD / ABSOLUTE.** When a major appendage class is mandatory or materially important across several characters, compare its architecture independently of colour/pattern. For every pair that shares that appendage class, require material divergence in at least **two** applicable structural dimensions among: count/cardinality where the canonical concept permits variation; body root/origin zone; skeletal/support integration; attachment depth and neighbouring-body deformation; planform/branching/segmentation; length-width-thickness/proportion; articulation/flexion; material-state construction; symmetry/asymmetry; topology/negative-space behavior; and silhouette role. A repeated paired-back-wing plan with only different membranes, feathers, colours, markings, or edge ornament fails. Where a concept fixes count or body plan, preserve that invariant and obtain divergence from the remaining permitted dimensions rather than violating the canonical leaf contract.

A strategy includes:

- body substrate;
- axial architecture;
- major proportion regime;
- major silhouette source;
- internal topology;
- appendage origin;
- concept-fusion mechanism;
- principal asymmetry.

Changing colour, hair, markings, or one isolated appendage does not create a new strategy.

#### 5.11.13 Five-way divergence matrix — HARD

Before certification, compare all five bodies simultaneously across these structural axes:

- stature/scale regime;
- head/body ratio;
- torso/ribcage architecture;
- chest architecture;
- shoulder architecture;
- waist architecture;
- hip/lower-torso architecture;
- limb architecture;
- skeletal/joint architecture;
- mass distribution;
- body substrate;
- internal topology;
- cavity/opening architecture;
- appendage origin and attachment;
- appendage morphology;
- asymmetry;
- dominant silhouette;
- dominant concept-fusion mechanism;
- material hierarchy;
- colour architecture.

The five solutions must not cluster around one common answer across most axes. If three or more characters share the same answer across several major axes, redesign the clustered solutions before rendering.

A change in pose, facial expression, hair styling, colour, markings, or decorative surface is never counted toward this divergence requirement.

#### 5.11.13A TEN-PAIR MACRO-BODY DIVERGENCE FLOOR — HARD / ABSOLUTE

For each of the ten unique character pairs, compare the bodies **before** colour, hair styling, markings, surface micro-detail, any separate coverage surrogate, or viewpoint-dependent presentation evidence is considered.

Every pair must differ materially across several independent macro systems. As a minimum floor, each pair must show clear non-cosmetic divergence in at least four of the following, with at least one difference belonging to central torso/shoulder/hip/limb architecture rather than only height or head scale:

- stature/overall scale regime;
- head-to-body ratio;
- ribcage/torso length, width, depth, and curvature;
- shoulder width/slope/topology;
- chest/waist construction;
- hip/lower-torso architecture and centre of mass;
- torso-to-leg and thigh-to-calf relationships;
- arm/leg length, thickness, taper, and joint placement;
- musculature/skeletal prominence/mass distribution;
- overall body silhouette independent of appendages.

An initial sheet group of five similarly tall, similarly long-legged, similarly narrow-waisted, similarly proportioned characters fails this gate even when horns, wings, tails, palettes, faces, or materials differ. Appendage diversity does not compensate for repeated underlying body architecture.

#### 5.11.14 Anti-tameness population gate — HARD

The population must contain visibly ambitious, structurally specific solutions. “Different” does not mean merely taller/shorter, lighter/darker, larger/smaller appendages, or different colours.

Every character must use a structurally specific complete solution, but there is no fixed quota requiring a prescribed number of major body systems to be dramatically transformed. A restrained construction may pass when the complete concept vocabulary and required interactions are already unmistakable, materially integrated, aesthetically strong, and genuinely different from the other characters. Population differentiation must still be architectural and multi-axis overall rather than superficial.

The population is invalid if the five silhouettes can be described as the same conventional humanoid body with different concept ornaments.

The population is also invalid if differentiation is achieved by making one member anatomically degraded or accidentally swollen as a shortcut. Extreme design must remain refined, controlled, intentionally proportioned, and main-character-grade; deliberate powerful or high-mass bodies remain valid.

Every candidate must still read as a heroine-grade flagship identity rather than as filler inside a concept batch. Distinctiveness must be carried by body regime, face/hair identity, concept-fusion architecture, appendage/topology logic, material hierarchy, and silhouette—not by one decorative gimmick attached to an otherwise generic body.

#### 5.11.15 Useful differentiation — HARD / ABSOLUTE

“Different” is not sufficient. Differentiation is useful only when the five characters represent **five materially different independently authored bodies under their frozen candidate-specific complete Resolved Primary Concept Sets**.

The population MUST therefore differ in both:

1. **body architecture**, and
2. **concept-combination materialization architecture**.

A population fails if the bodies are technically different but the concepts are materialized through essentially the same recipe in every character. It also fails when one primary concept visually blankets the character while the others survive only as token accents, local inserts, colour, or the same repeated support recipe, or when several characters use the same symbiosis logic with only relocated anatomy or recoloured materials.

Useful differentiation requires that the **causal organization of the complete concept fusion changes** between characters.

#### 5.11.16 Concept-fusion divergence — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Before body construction, each character MUST receive an independently resolved **Concept-Fusion Architecture** describing how the complete supplied concept set jointly constructs that particular character.

The five Concept-Fusion Architectures MUST be materially different. For every pair of characters, the frozen constructions must differ in at least three of the following core causal dimensions: primary fusion locus, dominant substrate/material-state organization, topology mechanism, appendage-root organization, complete-set higher-order carrier, and concept-concentration pattern. At least one of those differences must change the central body/substrate/topology/higher-order construction rather than only an appendage parameter.

For each character, resolve:

- principal body system/locus through which the combined concepts most strongly alter the character;
- secondary propagation systems where useful rather than as a quota;
- every distinct pairwise interaction for the actual concept-set cardinality;
- the complete-set higher-order interaction for n ≥ 3;
- meaningful intermediate higher-order interactions only when constructionally relevant;
- the region or system where fusion is most concentrated;
- distributed fusion regions where applicable;
- the shared geometry/topology/material behavior produced by interaction;
- appendage consequences where applicable;
- cavity/topology consequences where applicable;
- face/head/hair consequences where applicable;
- silhouette/focal-hierarchy mechanism;
- asymmetry mechanism where useful.

For A × B × C this includes A×B, A×C, B×C, and A×B×C. For any other cardinality, use the generalized pairwise/complete-set rules rather than inventing or omitting concepts.

For every supplied concept, the Frozen Character Specification must also resolve where that concept is strongest, where it participates secondarily if useful, and where it is intentionally quiet. Uniform whole-body repetition is not the default. Concentrating a concept strongly into selected body systems is valid and often preferable when it produces clearer material identity, stronger interaction, and a more memorable character, provided the complete concept remains unmistakable and causally necessary to the whole body. No character may reduce a concept to a small isolated token, but no character is required to spread every concept over every region.

No character may reuse another character's complete Concept-Fusion Architecture.

The complete concept set remains equally necessary in every character. Changing the fusion architecture does **not** permit weakening, omitting, or relegating any supplied concept, and it does not require maximum anatomical propagation.
##### 5.11.16.1 Different causal grammar — HARD

The five characters MUST NOT repeatedly solve the concepts with the same causal grammar.

The actual body-level carriers and their interactions must be reorganized between characters so that the their frozen complete Resolved Primary Concept Sets produce **independently authored anatomical/topological/material solutions**.

The concepts may appear through hair, eyes, torso, chest, waist, hip/lower-torso region, limbs, joints, skeleton, internal topology, cavities, apertures, substrate, markings when physically integrated, appendage roots, appendages, and silhouette, but the distribution and interaction of those systems MUST vary substantially between characters.

##### 5.11.16.2 Shared consequence requirement — HARD

Each character MUST contain clearly visible structures or systems that are consequences of **concept interaction**, not merely of individual concepts placed beside one another.

A shared consequence is valid when its final geometry, topology, material, morphology, placement, proportion, attachment, surface behavior, face/head/hair construction, or silhouette materially depends on at least two supplied concepts.

For every primary-concept pair, at least one genuine physical consequence must exist. For three or more concepts, at least one unmistakable complete-set higher-order construction must depend on all supplied concepts. That complete-set construction must alter a macro or strong-meso body system—substrate, central anatomy, major topology, appendage root/support, limb/torso architecture, or another comparably structural system. A small accent, seam, marking, glow line, isolated ornament, or surface-only intersection cannot serve as the sole higher-order construction.

There is **no requirement for multiple separate major shared structures** when one integrated construction already carries several interaction obligations convincingly. Additional shared structures are added only when they improve interaction readability, concept completeness, material identity, composition, or character appeal.

The same shared-consequence architecture MUST NOT be mechanically repeated across all five characters.
##### 5.11.16.3 Fusion-locus divergence — HARD

The five characters MUST materially vary where the concept combination changes the body. Across the population, the combined concepts must be capable of producing substantially different primary structural loci, including but not limited to:

- axial/skeletal construction;
- torso/ribcage/chest construction;
- waist/hip/lower-torso construction;
- limb/joint construction;
- internal topology/cavity construction;
- body substrate/material construction;
- appendage-root construction;
- distributed whole-body morphology;
- silhouette construction.

This is not a menu and does not require one locus per character. The requirement is that the population cannot repeatedly concentrate the same concept fusion in the same body systems.

##### 5.11.16.4 Concept-recognition after divergence — HARD

Changing the fusion architecture must not weaken the physical evidence for any concept. After the five mechanisms diverge, each body must still materially express the **same complete supplied concept combination** through its intact anatomy. Recognition is evaluated through the explicit authored interpretation and observable physical evidence, without labels, effects, environment, clothing, props, pose, or cosmetic explanation.

Thus the required state is:

> **SAME CONCEPT SET + DIFFERENT FUSION ARCHITECTURE + DIFFERENT BODY ARCHITECTURE + SAME COMPLETE CONCEPT RECOGNIZABILITY**

**INVALID:**

> **SAME CONCEPT SET + SAME FUSION RECIPE + COSMETIC/BODY VARIATIONS**

##### 5.11.16.5 Useful-difference independence test — HARD

For every pair of characters, remove their colour, hair styling, markings, presentation, pose, expression, and micro-detail. Then compare only:

- body architecture;
- concept-derived structures;
- concept interaction structures;
- topology;
- substrate/material;
- appendage roots and morphology;
- cavities/openings;
- asymmetry;
- silhouette.

If the pair still uses substantially the same concept-combination construction with superficial changes, the pair FAILS.

##### 5.11.16.6 Population fusion matrix — HARD

Before rendering, construct a five-row comparison with one row per character and at minimum these columns:

> **HEIGHT / BODY REGIME / PRIMARY FUSION LOCUS / SECONDARY FUSION SYSTEMS / MATERIAL-STATE ORGANIZATION / CONCEPT-CONCENTRATION PATTERN / A+B INTERACTION / A+C INTERACTION / B+C INTERACTION / A+B+C STRUCTURE / TOPOLOGY / SUBSTRATE / APPENDAGE ROOT ARCHITECTURE / SILHOUETTE MECHANISM / ASYMMETRY MECHANISM**

No two rows may be materially equivalent across the majority of these fields.

If two or more rows converge, rebuild the fusion architecture **before** rendering.

If three or more rows converge, discard the clustered fusion architecture and perform a new independent solution search for the affected characters.
##### 5.11.16.7 No feature-swapping differentiation — HARD

Do not create differentiation by swapping which isolated concept feature is larger, smaller, longer, shorter, darker, lighter, more numerous, or more decorative while retaining the same underlying fusion architecture.

The differentiation must change the **way the concepts construct the body**, not merely the parameters of repeated concept features.

##### 5.11.16.8 No concept-by-concept partitioning — HARD

The five characters MUST NOT divide the concepts into separate body regions in a repeated manner.

The desired construction is not:

> **CONCEPT A REGION + CONCEPT B REGION + CONCEPT C REGION**

The desired construction is:

> **CONCEPT A × CONCEPT B × CONCEPT C → INTERDEPENDENT BODY ARCHITECTURE**

Each concept remains individually visible, but its visible implementation must participate in shared physical structures with the other concepts.

#### 5.11.17 Five independent fusion constructions — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The population must contain five genuinely independent answers to the same complete concept-fusion problem.

Independence is evaluated at the level of causal body construction, not at the level of visual styling.

For each character, the compiler must independently resolve:

- which body systems become the primary carriers of the fused concepts;
- which body systems become secondary carriers;
- where the concepts physically intersect;
- how pairwise interactions alter anatomy;
- how higher-order interactions alter anatomy;
- where the body substrate changes;
- where topology changes;
- where cavities/openings occur;
- how appendages originate;
- how appendages integrate with the body;
- how mass is distributed;
- how proportions are established;
- how asymmetry operates;
- how the resulting silhouette is constructed.

No character may be generated by starting from another character and changing a limited collection of local parameters.

##### 5.11.17.1 Differentiation must be useful — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The purpose of population differentiation is not to make five images look superficially different. The purpose is to occupy five materially different regions of the physical solution space.

A useful difference is one that changes the actual answer to the question:

> **How does this complete concept combination physically become this female character?**

The answer must materially differ from the answers used for the other four characters.

The following dimensions must be considered simultaneously:

- height;
- body scale;
- head/body relationship;
- neck;
- shoulders;
- ribcage;
- torso length;
- waist;
- hip/lower-torso region;
- hips;
- chest volume;
- chest projection;
- chest placement;
- limb length;
- limb thickness;
- joint placement;
- musculature;
- skeletal architecture;
- body substrate;
- internal topology;
- cavity architecture;
- appendage count;
- appendage origin;
- appendage integration;
- appendage geometry;
- appendage material;
- concept-interaction structures;
- material architecture;
- silhouette;
- asymmetry.

The compiler must not select one or two dimensions and treat those as sufficient population differentiation.

##### 5.11.17.2 Height divergence is structural — HARD

Each five-character sheet group must permit genuinely large stature differences.

The compiler must be capable of resolving both very short and very tall female characters when those solutions are useful to the concept-fusion problem.

Height differences must propagate into actual body architecture rather than being produced by simple vertical scaling.

A tall character may require different:

- skeletal proportions;
- torso-to-leg relationship;
- limb architecture;
- shoulder geometry;
- hip/lower-torso geometry;
- mass distribution;
- appendage proportions;
- silhouette construction.

A short character must likewise be a fully resolved body rather than a compressed version of another character.

##### 5.11.17.3 Body-regime divergence is structural — HARD

The five characters must be capable of materially different body regimes—including petite, elongated, athletic, muscular, broad, soft, curvy, full-figured, powerful, and other coherent solutions—while remaining controlled, anatomically legible in the chosen stylized anatomy, aesthetically strong, and main-character-grade.

Body-regime differentiation includes, where useful:

- narrow versus broad skeletal architecture;
- compact versus elongated architecture;
- different torso construction;
- different shoulder-to-hip/lower-torso region relationships;
- different waist construction;
- different hip/lower-torso geometry;
- different hip architecture;
- different chest architecture;
- different limb-to-torso relationships;
- different musculature;
- different mass distribution;
- different structural density.

Body-regime variation must be selected because it creates a different physical solution to the fused concept problem, not because the population requires arbitrary visual variety.

##### 5.11.17.4 Chest-architecture divergence is part of body identity — HARD

Chest architecture is a structural component of female-character differentiation and must remain genuinely variable.

The compiler must consider variation in:

- volume;
- width;
- projection;
- placement;
- vertical position;
- relation to ribcage;
- relation to torso length;
- relation to waist;
- relation to shoulders;
- relation to hip/lower-torso region;
- integration with concept-derived body structures.

The five characters must not converge on the same chest construction while other minor structures are varied.

##### 5.11.17.5 Appendage divergence must be extreme when the concept permits it — HARD

Where a supplied concept's major recognizable vocabulary or the resolved fusion requires appendages or appendage-like morphology, the compiler must search broadly rather than converge on a familiar default. Required appendage classes remain present in every applicable character; their architecture is what diverges.

Across the five characters, appendage architecture may differ radically in:

- count;
- pairing;
- symmetry;
- asymmetry;
- origin;
- attachment depth;
- attachment orientation;
- length;
- thickness;
- branching;
- segmentation;
- articulation;
- material;
- internal structure;
- relationship to the skeleton;
- relationship to the torso;
- relationship to the limbs;
- interaction with cavities;
- interaction with substrate;
- interaction with other concept-derived structures;
- silhouette contribution.

A population containing five characters with essentially the same appendage origin and architecture but different size or colour fails useful differentiation.

##### 5.11.17.6 Concept-interaction divergence must be visible — HARD

The population must not merely vary where individual concepts appear. It must vary how the concepts interact.

For each character, resolve all pairwise interactions required by the actual concept-set cardinality and the complete-set higher-order interaction when n ≥ 3.

Each required interaction must have a physical implementation. The **population must not converge on one repeated interaction recipe**, but this does not require every pairwise mechanism to be unique in isolation. A particular pairwise idea may recur when its physical implementation, relation to the rest of the concept set, and complete character architecture are materially different.

The important comparison is the causal organization of the whole fusion. Repeated generic higher-order construction with altered styling fails.

For a three-concept command, A×B, A×C, B×C, and A×B×C are the explicit interaction set. For larger or smaller sets, use the generalized rule.

##### 5.11.17.7 No first-character contamination — HARD

Character 1 is not a template.

Character 1's body must not establish an implicit population baseline that later characters merely modify.

After Character 1 is resolved, the remaining characters must be independently searched against the complete unresolved concept-fusion problem.

Before rendering, compare each later character against all previously resolved bodies and inspect its own concept-decomposition and construction record. Verify that its major architecture was resolved from the complete primary combination, without borrowing an earlier body and changing parameters. Cite its independent structural decisions and concrete differences; an unsupported claim about what would have happened in an imagined design history is not evidence.

If the record or comparison shows inherited construction, the later planned character fails and must be independently resolved again before rendering.

##### 5.11.17.8 Population differentiation is a proof obligation — HARD

Before rendering, the compiler must be able to state for every character:

- what makes its body architecture distinct;
- what makes its concept-fusion architecture distinct;
- what makes its concept-interaction architecture distinct;
- what makes its appendage architecture distinct;
- what makes its material/substrate architecture distinct;
- what makes its silhouette mechanism distinct;
- what makes its asymmetry mechanism distinct.

These differences must remain meaningful after cosmetic and presentation variables are removed.

If the explanation depends on colour, hair styling, markings, pose, expression, micro-detail, or presentation rather than structural body differences, the differentiation proof fails.

#### 5.11.18 Identity divergence means independent characters, not parameter variation — HARD / ABSOLUTE

The population requirement is stronger than height variation, colour variation, appendage-count variation, or isolated anatomy variation.

Each of the five characters MUST be constructed as an independently authored female anime/manhwa character identity produced from each character's frozen complete Resolved Primary Concept Set. They must not read as members of one base character family, alternate skins of one body, or five parameterized versions of a single construction.

The compiler MUST treat the following as independent identity systems rather than as optional variation sliders:

1. cranial architecture;
2. face geometry;
3. eye geometry;
4. eyebrow geometry;
5. nose and mouth construction;
6. jaw and cheek architecture;
7. hair mass architecture;
8. hair framing of the face;
9. neck construction;
10. shoulder architecture;
11. ribcage architecture;
12. torso length and topology;
13. waist architecture;
14. hip/lower-torso region architecture;
15. hip architecture;
16. chest architecture;
17. limb proportion;
18. limb construction;
19. joint architecture;
20. musculature;
21. body-mass distribution;
22. body substrate;
23. internal topology;
24. aperture topology;
25. appendage architecture;
26. appendage origin;
27. appendage count and pairing;
27. appendage material;
27. asymmetry mechanism;
27. dominant silhouette;
27. concept-fusion locus;
27. pairwise concept interactions;
27. higher-order concept interaction;
27. surface architecture;
27. material architecture;
36. colour architecture;
37. character-specific character-identity finishing.

A character may share a concept-derived mechanism with another character only when the physical implementation is independently resolved and materially different. Shared concepts do not authorize shared construction.

##### 5.11.18.1 No inherited visual grammar — HARD

Character N MUST NOT inherit the previous character's:

- head shape;
- face construction;
- eye construction;
- hair silhouette;
- shoulder-to-hip/lower-torso region ratio;
- torso architecture;
- waist construction;
- hip/lower-torso region construction;
- chest architecture;
- limb grammar;
- appendage grammar;
- aperture placement logic;
- material/substrate logic;
- silhouette logic;
- asymmetry logic;
- concept-fusion mechanism.

A different colour, hairstyle, appendage count, or body height does not reset inherited visual grammar.

##### 5.11.18.2 Independence must remain after finishing/colour stripping — HARD

Conceptually suppress colour coding, hair styling, markings, surface decoration, micro-detail, and presentation as evidence of differentiation. Keep constituent body matter and structurally necessary anatomy intact.

The five underlying bodies must still read as five separately authored physical identities.

If two bodies become effectively the same person with different surface treatment, the population fails.

##### 5.11.18.3 Independence with concept labels hidden — HARD

Hide the concept names and explanations while keeping the actual anatomy, topology, constituent materials, and appendages intact. Compare the two complete constructions. They must still be visibly different bodies.

Do not remove concept-derived anatomy to reveal a supposed underlying conventional mannequin: the fusion is the body’s origin. The test removes explanatory labels, not body structures.

##### 5.11.18.4 No random-parameter differentiation — HARD

Differentiation MUST NOT be produced by arbitrary random changes detached from the design problem.

Every major difference must have an aesthetic and structural purpose inside that character's independently resolved concept fusion.

The compiler must be able to explain why a specific height, torso ratio, chest construction, aperture topology, appendage architecture, asymmetry pattern, material system, facial design, or colour architecture belongs to that particular character.

The answer must be a physical-design consequence, not “variation was needed.”

##### 5.11.18.5 Independent-character aesthetic completion — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Every one of the five characters MUST be resolved to a complete main-character-grade design before the sheet is certified.

A character is not complete merely because its body is structurally different.

Each character must possess a deliberate and internally coherent combination of:

- face identity;
- eye identity;
- hair identity;
- body identity;
- concept-fusion identity;
- appendage identity;
- material identity;
- colour identity;
- asymmetry identity;
- silhouette identity;
- fine surface/character-identity finishing.

The five characters must therefore be independently strong enough that any one of them could serve as the principal character design of a polished anime/manhwa production.

No character may be treated as a secondary variant whose reduced face development, simplified hair, simplified surface design, weaker material hierarchy, weaker colour architecture, or weaker structural refinement is justified by its position in the sheet.

##### 5.11.18.6 Character 01 is not privileged — HARD

Character 01 receives no higher design-resolution budget than Characters 02–05.

All five characters require:

- equally deliberate facial construction;
- equally deliberate body architecture;
- equally deliberate concept fusion;
- equally deliberate appendage construction;
- equally deliberate material design;
- equally deliberate colour design;
- equally deliberate asymmetry;
- equally deliberate micro-detail;
- equally deliberate finish.

The first character cannot consume the majority of the available design complexity.

##### 5.11.18.7 No family-template convergence — HARD

The following population state is invalid:

> same head + same face + same torso + same limbs + same appendage roots + same aperture logic + different height/colour/details.

The following population state is also invalid:

> same body + different concept feature placement + different cosmetic treatment.

The desired state is:

> five independently resolved female characters whose physical identities, concept-fusion architectures, aesthetic systems, and detailed finishes are independently authored from the complete concept set.

#### 5.11.19 Face and character-design development — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Face quality is part of the main-character quality floor. The face MUST NOT be a generic anime placeholder attached to an otherwise elaborate body.

For every character, independently resolve:

- skull/head shape;
- forehead proportion;
- brow shape and placement;
- eye size;
- eye width and height;
- eye spacing;
- upper and lower eyelid geometry;
- iris geometry;
- pupil geometry;
- iris internal structure;
- highlight structure;
- eyebrow design;
- nose construction;
- mouth construction;
- lip shape;
- cheek geometry;
- jaw geometry;
- chin geometry;
- ear geometry where visible;
- facial plane transitions;
- hairline;
- face-framing hair architecture;
- facial surface/material treatment;
- character-specific cosmetic detailing.

The exact face solution MUST be different between characters at the level of geometry and identity, not merely eye colour or hairstyle.

##### 5.11.19.1 Face must be readable at sheet scale — HARD

The face must remain sufficiently developed to establish character identity at the intended sheet viewing scale.

Micro-detail may refine the eyes and facial surfaces, but the primary facial geometry must already be resolved.

A face that becomes distinctive only after extreme enlargement fails the face-quality gate.

##### 5.11.19.2 Face must participate in the concept fusion — HARD

Where the concepts permit physical facial consequences, the face/head system must participate in the complete fusion.

Creature-derived cranial structures, altered eye architecture, integrated surface structures, asymmetrical facial morphology, material transitions, aperture/topology relationships, or other directly physical consequences may be used when supported by the resolved concept interaction.

These must remain part of the female character rather than replacing the face with a creature head, mask, object, or abstract symbol.

##### 5.11.19.3 Character-identity finishing is an active design layer — HARD

Phase 1 requires deliberate character-identity finishing intrinsic to the character.

The compiler MUST actively resolve appropriate identity systems such as:

- distinctive eye rendering and iris design;
- eyelid and eyebrow design;
- lip/mouth design;
- hairline and hair-shape language;
- face-framing hair architecture;
- controlled hair highlights;
- pigmentation or facial markings where selected;
- facial material transitions;
- nail/fingertip treatment where selected and visible;
- line-weight emphasis around identity-bearing facial structures;
- optional makeup such as eyeliner, eyeshadow, blush, or lip colour when it strengthens the exact character.

These systems are subordinate to the body and concept architecture but required collectively at the **identity** level so that the five characters do not become unfinished mannequins. No particular cosmetic item is mandatory.

A no-makeup character is fully valid when the underlying face/head/eye/hair/material identity is complete. Identity finishing must strengthen an already distinct character and cannot disguise structural convergence.

##### 5.11.19.4 Identity finishing must be character-specific — HARD

No two characters should receive the same face/head/hair finishing recipe by default.

Vary, where appropriate and aesthetically justified:

- face geometry;
- eye geometry;
- iris architecture;
- brow geometry;
- mouth/lip geometry;
- facial line hierarchy;
- hair silhouette;
- hair volume distribution;
- hair directionality;
- facial surface/material treatment;
- pigmentation or integrated markings;
- optional makeup treatment;
- highlight placement.

Variation must be deliberate rather than random. For each initial five-character sheet group, the Frozen Character Specifications must resolve five different combinations of face geometry, eye architecture, hair silhouette, hair volume distribution, hair direction/texture, pigmentation or integrated markings where used, and optional cosmetic treatment where useful. Recolouring the same face-and-hair construction does not create a new identity.
#### 5.11.20 Colour and material identity divergence — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Colour is not sufficient for structural differentiation, but colour and material are still required for complete character identity.

The five characters MUST NOT collapse into one shared palette family unless the concept solution independently requires it and the individual material architectures remain strongly differentiated. The default requirement is deliberate palette divergence.

Each character receives an independently resolved colour architecture containing:

- dominant hue family;
- secondary hue family;
- accent hue family;
- value hierarchy;
- saturation hierarchy;
- light/dark mass distribution;
- material-to-colour relationship;
- body-substrate colour;
- appendage colour/material relationship;
- aperture/void interior treatment;
- hair colour architecture;
- eye colour architecture;
- controlled focal accents.

##### 5.11.20.1 Palette divergence is architectural — HARD

Do not merely rotate the same three colours between characters.

Do not preserve the same dominant-light-body / dark-appendage / bright-accent recipe across the population.

The colour architecture itself must differ in:

- where the darkest masses occur;
- where the lightest masses occur;
- which structures carry saturation;
- which structures are muted;
- which materials contrast strongly;
- how accents travel through the silhouette;
- how hair relates to the body substrate;
- how appendages relate to the torso;
- how aperture interiors relate to surrounding structures.

##### 5.11.20.2 Material architecture must be character-specific — HARD

Material differentiation must alter how the body is visually constructed, not merely change its colour.

Resolve independently:

- substrate character;
- surface roughness language;
- gloss level;
- translucency language;
- opacity relationships;
- structural density impression;
- surface segmentation;
- material boundary shape;
- transition between materials;
- relationship between body substrate and appendages.

The rendering remains drawn 2D. Material richness is expressed through illustration, linework, shape design, and controlled value grouping rather than physically simulated rendering. Each materially distinct substance must receive its own illustrated response where applicable: shadow edge/softness, highlight geometry and intensity, edge sharpness or breakup, granularity, roughness/smoothness cues, opacity/translucency, internal pattern, fracture/cleavage logic, fibrous direction, layered overlap, porosity, membrane thinness, liquid flow behavior, local contrast, and transition logic. Two materials may share similar colours and still must remain visually distinguishable through the way they are drawn.

When a supplied primary concept is itself a material or substance, its Frozen Character Specification must resolve three things together: **physical state**, **characteristic material behaviour**, and **the macroscopic body geometry/topology produced by those properties**. The state must affect at least one macro or strong-meso body system such as substrate, internal volume, limb/torso construction, appendage construction, cavity/channel construction, or a major material transition. A liquid-like, viscous, granular, crystalline, fibrous, porous, layered, suspended, brittle, ductile, or other resolved state must behave as that state in the body rather than being translated automatically into the same rigid plate/shell grammar. Colour, value, sheen, polish, glow, surface finish, or painted pattern alone cannot satisfy materialization. If mentally removing colour and highlight treatment leaves the same geometry as a generic solid body, the material concept is under-materialized unless the resolved state is genuinely solid and that geometry is specifically justified. Materialization must also be aesthetically authored: state-accurate behavior that produces an arbitrary blob, generic growth, or visually unresolved insertion does not pass premium-character quality. The material consequence must contribute to an intentional silhouette, focal hierarchy, transition rhythm, or identity-bearing body structure.
#### 5.11.21 Universal aperture / cavity / internal-topology divergence engine — HARD / ABSOLUTE
