The design process must consider the complete useful clothing space before committing to the final architecture. Candidate directions may include soft garments, rigid garments, armor, partial armor, ceremonial construction, fitted construction, layered construction, draped construction, asymmetric construction, coat or cape systems, harness systems, bodice systems, skirt or panel systems, legwear systems, wearable mechanical structures, or coherent hybrids.

Category selection is subordinate to character-specific fitness. The final outfit must be the resolved answer for this character rather than the most obvious costume associated with a concept label.

#### 6.3.1 Operational explanation

Explore multiple useful clothing families without importing a stereotypical era or profession. The chosen sub-concepts influence construction, not narrative role.

#### 6.3.2 Worked application

One candidate can combine a fitted central garment with wide detached sleeves and a structured waist; another can use asymmetric armor and split draped panels. Neither is selected merely because the concept word suggests a stock costume.

#### 6.3.3 Failure and validation

Confirm distinct architecture rather than five copies of a bodice or coat. No fixed armor, gown, fantasy era, or palette applies universally.

### 6.4 Badassness and open-region architecture co-dominance

Badassness and deliberate open-region architecture are co-dominant Phase-2 design objectives. They are not opposing categories and must be fused as strongly as the character-specific design permits.

The default target is a visually powerful, imposing, memorable outfit whose garment geometry deliberately frames selected body regions. Power must come from authored construction rather than indiscriminate coverage, and openness must come from designed boundaries rather than missing design.

Open central-torso architecture and high-cut lower edges on upper-torso garment structures are explicit preferred coverage parameters. They must be considered during outfit resolution and integrated whenever they coexist coherently with the character, selected garment architecture, and visual hierarchy.

Open-region architecture must be produced by actual garment geometry. Applicable mechanisms include:

- upper-torso structures with deliberately high lower edges;
- open abdominal construction;
- separated upper and lower garment masses leaving a visible central torso region;
- central, lateral, or asymmetric torso openings;
- waist framing;
- hip or upper-thigh openings;
- split panels;
- open coats, mantles, capes, or drapes that frame visible anatomy;
- straps, harnesses, rigid borders, articulated plates, or fitted structures that define open regions through complete support and attachment logic.

Badassness must likewise be physicalized through concrete wearable construction rather than adjectives. Applicable mechanisms include:

- strong shoulder architecture;
- architectural collars;
- rigid or semi-rigid panels;
- articulated armor;
- engineered harnesses;
- layered garment masses;
- structured coats, capes, mantles, or drapes;
- strong closures and attachment systems;
- silhouette-defining garment geometry;
- substantial material contrasts;
- protective plates;
- gauntlets or arm structures;
- strong boot or leg architecture;
- deliberate asymmetry;
- controlled large-scale garment masses.

A successful outfit may therefore be simultaneously heavily authored, imposing, armored, ceremonial, elegant, severe, or monumental while retaining broad deliberate open regions around the central torso, waist, hips, thighs, or other character-specific areas. Open-region architecture must not reduce the outfit to disconnected scraps, and imposing construction must not bury the frozen body beneath shapeless bulk.

#### 6.4.1 Operational explanation

Badassness and open-region architecture operate together through authored physical geometry. Covered and open regions are designed as one system; greater visible area alone is not greater design quality.

#### 6.4.2 Worked application

A substantial asymmetric shoulder and gauntlet system can balance a framed open abdomen and articulated hip panels. Strong collar/closure construction and controlled hanging masses sustain an imposing silhouette while leaving deliberate visual breathing room around the body.

#### 6.4.3 Failure and validation

Check concrete imposing geometry and intentional open-region architecture simultaneously. Neither indiscriminate full coverage nor disconnected minimal scraps automatically fulfils the objective.

### 6.5 Open-region architecture quality gate

Open-region architecture is evaluated as part of outfit architecture, not as an independent decoration.

- Open regions must have clear designed boundaries.
- The outfit must visibly frame the body regions left open.
- The visible body remains the certified Phase-1 body rather than being altered to suit an opening.
- Open central-torso construction should preserve readable waist, abdomen, and torso architecture.
- A high lower edge on an upper-torso garment must be produced by that garment's actual boundary and support architecture.
- Open-region geometry must participate in the outfit's silhouette, layering, rhythm, asymmetry, or material distribution.
- Large open regions do not excuse weak garment construction.
- Small open regions do not satisfy the objective when the design can coherently support a stronger open-region solution.
- Coverage must be intentional rather than accidental.

#### 6.5.1 Operational explanation

Open areas must have coherent borders, support, and relation to the body. They are deliberate parts of the garment's coverage map, not unfinished gaps.

#### 6.5.2 Worked application

An upper garment terminates along a defined lower edge while side supports connect to an open waist frame; the central torso region remains visible and a defining flank aperture stays fully clear of those supports.

#### 6.5.3 Failure and validation

Inspect actual boundary, support, front/side/rear coverage, and frozen-body preservation. A painted opening or altered body is invalid.

### 6.6 Outfit concept and sub-concept adaptation

The complete frozen character remains the source of outfit adaptation. The primary concept combination has already created the body and must not be re-assigned to clothing as though Phase 1 had failed.

Phase 2 may nevertheless echo, extend, contrast, frame, or structurally respond to established concepts through wearable construction. The two Phase-1 sub-concepts become explicit Phase-2 design inputs and must materially influence the outfit where applicable: Sub-concept 1 contributes its frozen actual Era / Culture / Civilization vocabulary under §5.10.1, while Sub-concept 2 contributes its randomly selected additional concept from a subordinate position.

Concept and sub-concept influence should be expressed through concrete visual systems such as garment geometry, coverage, component relationships, material choice, material distribution, attachment, layering, asymmetry, silhouette, surface treatment, ornament hierarchy, and color architecture.

A symbol, logo, printed motif, color swap, or isolated ornament is insufficient when it is the sole carrier of an assigned influence.

#### 6.6.1 Operational explanation

The primary combination already exists in the body. Clothing translates the two subordinate influences more strongly while responding to the whole established character.

#### 6.6.2 Worked application

A medieval, Persian, cyberpunk, mythological, invented-world, or other resolved Sub-concept-1 vocabulary can shape garment construction and ornament rhythm, while the actual randomly selected Sub-concept-2 concept can alter material behavior, component geometry, layering, openings, or another concrete clothing system. A logo or stereotypical token on otherwise generic clothing does not suffice.

#### 6.6.3 Failure and validation

Trace each assigned influence to garment geometry, material, attachment, layering, or coverage; verify that no missing body concept is reassigned to clothing.

### 6.7 Outfit structural resolution

Before generation, every outfit must be explicitly resolved across as many applicable structural dimensions as necessary:

- garment category and garment-family composition;

- component count and component hierarchy;

- primary silhouette and secondary silhouette masses;

- coverage distribution and open-region distribution;

- front, side, and rear coverage logic;

- layer count, layer order, overlap, separation, and negative space;

- upper-torso construction;

- upper-torso lower-edge construction;

- central-torso opening construction;

- waist and hip/lower-torso region construction;

- hip and thigh construction;

- sleeves and arm structures;

- collars and neck structures;

- shoulder structures;

- bodices, torso plates, chest structures, or harness structures where applicable;

- belts and waist attachment systems;

- skirts, split skirts, hanging panels, loin panels, or analogous lower-body structures;

- coats, capes, mantles, trains, drapes, or rear masses;

- legwear;

- boots or footwear;

- gloves, gauntlets, or hand structures;

- headwear when applicable;

- armor and protective structures;

- wearable technology or mechanical structures when applicable;

- closures, fasteners, buckles, clasps, lacing, wraps, straps, hinges, joints, or anchors;

- asymmetry and deliberate left/right differences;

- material allocation;

- rigidity versus drape;

- transparency, translucency, opacity, gloss, matte behavior, reflectivity, or luminosity where applicable;

- seams, edges, trims, borders, engraved surfaces, motifs, and ornamentation;

- color count, color hierarchy, color distribution, and accent placement.

#### 6.7.1 Operational explanation

Resolve both large clothing masses and their actual construction before renderer-description materialization. Every listed dimension is conditional on the selected garment, not a requirement to add every garment type.

#### 6.7.2 Worked application

A split coat has a resolved collar, front closure, sleeve form, rear panels, material thickness, hem lengths, and openings for the body’s actual appendages. Record absent boots or headwear rather than inventing them.

#### 6.7.3 Failure and validation

Confirm complete inventory, front/side/rear logic, joins, material allocation, palette, and coherent hierarchy. “Elaborate armor” is insufficient.

### 6.8 Clothing/body separation

Dedicated clothing must remain visibly wearable and physically distinguishable from anatomy. The viewer must be able to understand where the body ends and the garment begins.

Clothing/body separation may be established through seams, edges, thickness, layering, overlap, straps, attachment points, wrapping, draping, rigid panels, material transitions, closures, and garment-specific geometry.

Clothing must not accidentally become skin, replace skin, become a body substrate, merge into concept-derived anatomy, or convert an intrinsic Phase-1 structure into an external garment.

#### 6.8.1 Operational explanation

Body and clothing retain distinct ownership even when their colours or materials are similar. A metallic body may wear metal-like clothing, but edge thickness and attachment must distinguish the garment.

#### 6.8.2 Worked application

A rigid chest frame sits above an intrinsic plated torso with a readable edge, clearance, and fastener system. Its removal would leave the full certified anatomy intact.

#### 6.8.3 Failure and validation

Reject skin becoming cloth, body plates becoming removable armor without authorization, or garments merging ambiguously into anatomical apertures.

### 6.9 Unusual-anatomy accommodation

Unusual anatomy must be accommodated deliberately rather than hidden or ignored.

For every appendage, aperture, cavity, protrusion, wing, tail, horn, unusual limb, transformed region, or other nonstandard structure, the outfit must resolve whether it passes through, passes around, remains visible, receives a dedicated opening, receives an attachment interface, or causes a local garment restructuring.

The solution must preserve anatomical origin and topology. A garment opening cannot relocate the appendage or make its body connection ambiguous.

#### 6.9.1 Operational explanation

Accommodate unusual anatomy through the garment itself. Defining anatomical features remain visible rather than merely existing beneath coverage.

#### 6.9.2 Worked application

A tail exits through a resolved rear opening; a shoulder appendage receives clear root clearance; a side aperture is framed by separate upper and lower garment boundaries without a strap crossing its defining interior.

#### 6.9.3 Failure and validation

Check each anatomical feature against a coverage/clearance map. A cape can be arranged around a root, but it cannot erase the root’s visible relationship to the body.

### 6.10 Material integrity

Outfit materials may be conventional or unconventional, including fabric, leather, metal, crystal, glass-like material, translucent material, rigid composites, liquid-like coherent material, smoke-like coherent material, luminous material, energy-like coherent material, ethereal material, mechanical structures, or other visually coherent wearable substrates.

When a material is structural, it must visibly constitute the garment or component. It cannot exist only as a glow, shader, coating, decorative overlay, or detached effect.

Material behavior must remain readable in 2D through drawn edge behavior, fold behavior, thickness, highlights, shadows, transparency, texture, segmentation, and controlled surface treatment.

#### 6.10.1 Operational explanation

Unconventional material is eligible only when it constitutes a coherent wearable system with defined edges and attachment.

#### 6.10.2 Worked application

A translucent mantle has illustrated thickness, folds, layered boundaries, and a collar attachment. A luminous panel has a resolved shape and support; surrounding light is not the garment.

#### 6.10.3 Failure and validation

Check structural material presence and 2D depiction. Glow, coating, or atmospheric matter alone cannot replace construction.

### 6.11 Outfit differentiation gate

When more than one outfit is created, every candidate must be structurally distinct. Recoloring, motif substitution, accessory substitution, trim changes, or minor panel changes do not constitute a new outfit.

Cross-candidate differentiation must compare garment category, component architecture, silhouette, coverage map, open-region map, layering, shoulder architecture, torso architecture, waist/hip architecture, lower-body construction, major material distribution, asymmetry, and major attachment systems.

A new candidate that substantially repeats an already occupied clothing architecture must be redesigned before generation.

#### 6.11.1 Operational explanation

Candidate independence concerns garment architecture. Recolouring and accessory swaps are finishing changes rather than new clothing solutions.

#### 6.11.2 Worked application

Compare a fitted sleeveless garment with separated arm structures against an asymmetric coat/armor hybrid and a layered draped system. Different categories still require actual character-specific fit.

#### 6.11.3 Failure and validation

For ten candidates, compare all 45 pairs and inspect clusters for repeated coverage, shoulder, torso, skirt, and attachment recipes.

### 6.12 Character dominance and visual hierarchy

The outfit must enhance the frozen character rather than replace her identity.

- The body silhouette must remain meaningfully readable.

- Identity-defining anatomy must remain visibly readable. Clothing must be constructed around defining apertures, cavities, roots, and morphological features; visual coverage is not excused by leaving the hidden anatomy technically intact.

- The outfit cannot become the sole memorable feature.

- Large garment masses must support rather than overwhelm the character.

- Open-region architecture, armor, ornament, and drapery must participate in one hierarchy.

- Simple macro / intricate micro applies: major garment masses must read immediately; fine detail must remain subordinate.

#### 6.12.1 Operational explanation

Clothing can add substantial visual mass while keeping the character and defining body morphology dominant.

#### 6.12.2 Worked application

Large sleeves and a rear mantle frame a compact torso and visible flank aperture. Their volume is organized into two or three clear masses rather than a fringe of equally large competing panels.

#### 6.12.3 Failure and validation

Reject silhouettes overwhelmed by garments, concealed identity-defining anatomy, or fine ornament replacing strong primary construction.

### 6.13 Phase-2 2D-native rendering lock

Phase 2 is strictly 2D. The outfit must be designed and serialized for the active 2D anime/manhwa rendering language.

- Use clean illustrated contour hierarchy.

- Use graphic, readable garment shapes.

- Use controlled 2D shading and drawn highlights.

- Render metal, fabric, leather, crystal, and unusual materials as illustrated materials rather than PBR surfaces.

- Preserve clear edges between body, garment layers, and materials.

- Do not allow CGI, 3D-render, photorealistic, game-render, plastic, or physically based material drift.

- Micro-detail must not create noisy pseudo-3D texture.

#### 6.13.1 Operational explanation

The embedded 2D grammar applies to every outfit and display form. Drawn gloss and soft gradients are allowed when visibly illustrated.

#### 6.13.2 Worked application

Render a scale-like shoulder component with crisp drawn unit groups, a controlled dark base plane, and selected pale edge highlights. Do not imitate ray-traced metal.

#### 6.13.3 Failure and validation

Inspect contours, garment edges, folds, shadows, highlights, and material transitions; an anime head does not certify a 3D-looking costume.

### 6.14 Phase-2 design resolution gate

No outfit may be sent to generation until the following are resolved:

- frozen-character preservation;

- garment category and complete component inventory;

- badass/open-region fusion strategy;

- central-torso opening and upper-torso lower-edge treatment;

- front/side/rear coverage logic;

- character-specific anatomical accommodation;

- sub-concept adaptation;

- silhouette;

- layering and attachment;

- material architecture;

- color architecture;

- macro hierarchy;

- micro-detail hierarchy;

- 2D rendering translation;

- all applicable exclusions and preservation constraints.

If any mandatory design decision is unresolved, STOP → RESOLVE → REVALIDATE → CONTINUE.

#### 6.14.1 Operational explanation

The design-resolution gate is mandatory and precedes the Frozen Specification / Clean Renderer-Facing Description fidelity gate. Missing details cannot be delegated to the renderer.

#### 6.14.2 Worked application

An unresolved rear fastening or unknown aperture clearance blocks generation even if the front concept is attractive.

#### 6.14.3 Failure and validation

Record evidence for every applicable field. N/A requires an actual absence or inapplicability reason; vague confidence is not PASS.

### 6.14A Phase-2 deterministic outfit blueprint — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Before any Phase-2 READY TO RENDER certification, materialize each outfit candidate as one complete deterministic outfit blueprint. Internally resolve, as applicable: frozen-body revision and protected interfaces; garment category and macro silhouette; complete component inventory and counts; dimensions or fixed body-relative ratios where they materially affect construction; attachment, closure and support; layer order and overlap; exact front/profile/rear garment geometry; clearances around body-owned structures; material allocation, thickness, stiffness/flexibility and opacity; colour topology; seams, hems, openings and fold groups; asymmetry and repeated-unit organization; character/concept adaptation; and cross-view continuity.

The blueprint fixes one actual clothing solution. The renderer must not invent missing garment components, rear construction, fit, attachment, material boundaries, or layer relationships. An independent illustrator using the frozen body plus this blueprint must not face materially different plausible garment constructions.

**Internal/renderer boundary.** The internal blueprint may be rich. The Clean Renderer-Facing Description and FINAL RENDER CONTEXT remain shortest-lossless visible garment constructions with no word-count minimum: retain exact counts, dimensions, interfaces and geometry where they materially change the clothing, while leaving fit audits, proof bookkeeping and invisible dependencies internal. Renderer wording describes what the clothing physically is and where its edges/components are, not the internal rationale for choosing them. This additive blueprint never changes Phase-2 ownership: the frozen Phase-1 body is immutable input and only the outfit is authored.

### 6.15 Phase-2 generator handoff

Phase 2 enters generator handoff only after the outfit is complete final-refined render-ready state. The fully resolved current revision is frozen internally; a public written outfit specification is optional and shown only when the user asks for it.

Compile a positive Clean Renderer-Facing Description that preserves the frozen character, complete garment architecture, materials, closures, attachment logic, unusual-anatomy accommodation, silhouette, and every identity-bearing visible relationship. Internal goals such as `open-region architecture` are translated into **specific conventional garment geometry** before handoff: exact cutouts, open side panels, open-back structures, high slits, cropped layers, asymmetric gaps, articulated armor spacing, or other resolved garment boundaries. The renderer receives the actual garment, not an abstract objective about how much of the body should remain open.

Every renderer-facing outfit remains a complete coherent clothing system with explicit component boundaries, support, closure, and fit. Bold open-region design remains fully eligible, but it is expressed through garment construction rather than body-area objectives. The renderer does not choose missing garment category, garment openness, armor distribution, materials, attachment, or character adaptation.

Run the same renderer preflight principles as Phase 1: design completeness, body/outfit consistency, defining-morphology preservation, conventional positive garment serialization, description clarity, exact output geometry, and full character extent. Then run the Renderer Capability Gate. A SUPPORTED result enters READY TO RENDER; image submission still requires an explicit user `RENDER` command.
#### 6.15.1 Operational explanation

The internal outfit specification may remain exhaustive. The renderer-facing description is shorter and visual-only. It removes design proof, open-region scoring language, duplicated constraints, and body-area objectives while preserving every macro and strong-meso garment decision that defines the candidate. The handoff names concrete garment pieces and boundaries instead of explaining the design intent behind their openness.

#### 6.15.2 Worked application

A candidate with asymmetric shoulder support, open side-torso structure, articulated leg elements, and character-specific tail clearance is frozen with exact materials, closures, overlap, and attachment. The renderer-facing block states those visible constructions directly and preserves the exact frozen character beneath them.

#### 6.15.3 Failure and validation

If the preflight finds unresolved fit, hidden defining anatomy, contradictory garment layers, or omitted renderer-critical geometry, correct the outfit specification or serializer before submission. Post-render deviations are recorded against the frozen design rather than silently redefining it.

### 6.16 Phase-2 post-generation audit

After generation, inspect the actual image rather than assuming renderer-description conformance.

- Is the frozen character still the same character?

- Were body proportions and unusual anatomy preserved?

- Does the outfit visibly fit this specific body?

- Are badassness and deliberate open-region architecture simultaneously present where designed?

- Are central-torso opening and upper-torso lower-edge structures correctly realized?

- Is the garment architecture complete and coherent?

- Are clothing and anatomy visually distinct?

- Are appendage openings and accommodations correct?

- Are materials structural rather than decorative effects?

- Is the macro silhouette readable?

- Is micro-detail subordinate?

- Is the result genuinely 2D?

- Did the generator omit, merge, invent, or relocate any major garment component?

A failure of a mandatory property invalidates the generated outfit. Record FAILED, preserve the image unchanged, and perform no correction or retry. Do not certify a partially compliant result. Continue only with remaining authorized work in the active phase.

#### 6.16.1 Operational explanation

Post-generation inspection records actual conformance. It does not authorize a corrected render.

#### 6.16.2 Worked application

If the garment covers the certified side aperture or replaces the body’s shoulder root, record FAILED with that specific evidence and keep the image unchanged.

#### 6.16.3 Failure and validation

No automatic regeneration or retouch follows. Continue only with remaining authorized work in the active phase.

### 6.17 Phase-2 certification and freeze

An outfit becomes frozen only after it passes the Phase-2 design-resolution gate and post-generation audit and the user explicitly adopts and freezes it. The frozen outfit becomes an immutable input to Phase 3 and Phase 4 unless the user explicitly requests a targeted revision.

Certification freezes garment architecture, coverage map, open-region map, major components, material allocation, color architecture, attachment logic, silhouette, and identity-defining outfit details.

#### 6.17.1 Operational explanation

Passing technical inspection, user selection, and freeze are separate states. Freeze preserves the selected garment for downstream use without ending Phase 2.

#### 6.17.2 Worked application

The user may fuse two passing outfits, alter the composite, compare it, and then freeze the chosen result. Only an explicit command starts Phase 3.

#### 6.17.3 Failure and validation

Record the chosen outfit ID, exact revision, corresponding frozen body, and fit certificate; never consume a stale garment revision.

### 6.18 Ten-candidate outfit catalogue

#### 6.18.1 Rule and layout

Create ten structurally distinct outfits for each selected frozen character when an initial outfit catalogue is requested. Arrange them in two rows of five on one continuous 16:9 plain-white sheet. Use headless neutral display forms proportioned to that actual character where support is needed to communicate fit. The outfit is the subject; the display form is not a new character.

#### 6.18.2 Explanation

Every outfit must account for the actual frozen anatomy even when the character herself is not depicted. Use necessary display-form openings or topology to demonstrate accommodation. Do not add faces, independent hairstyles, poses, body redesigns, weapons, or scene elements. Display forms are analytical supports, not prohibited concept-bearing floating objects. All outfit components must be shown uncropped, including sleeves, trains, footwear, rear masses visible in the selected display orientation, and headwear displayed coherently if part of the resolved outfit.

Each candidate has one principal catalogue depiction. Do not import the Phase-1 five-front population layout automatically. Front, side, and rear garment construction must nevertheless be resolved in the blueprint; choose a fixed analytical display angle that clearly presents the garment. Additional detail outputs require user authorization and must not replace the ten-candidate catalogue.

Rows are spatial organization only. Both badassness and open-region objectives apply to every candidate. A row label cannot permit weak construction or abandonment of either objective. Candidate numbers may be used minimally for selection; do not invent narrative names, titles, or descriptions. No decorative dividers, sample palette strips, scene backgrounds, or black screenshot borders are required.

#### 6.18.3 Worked application

A character with a side aperture receives ten dedicated clothing constructions. Each neutral display form preserves the relevant torso proportions and the aperture's location. One outfit frames it with an open side panel; another routes separate supports above and below; another uses an offset closure and rear-supported drape. All ten must differ through complete garment architecture, not only the opening shape.

#### 6.18.4 Failure and validation

Ten similar white-blue gowns with varied trim do not pass. Cropped upper-row hems, hidden rear attachment assumptions, an anonymous generic mannequin that changes fit, or a 3D catalogue render fail independently. Compare all 45 candidate pairs and confirm category, coverage, layering, attachment, material, palette, macro hierarchy, complete extent, and correct embedded 2D treatment.

### 6.19 Outfit design vocabulary and preference application

#### 6.19.1 Rule

Explore a broad clothing vocabulary while maintaining character-specific adaptation, deliberate open-region architecture, strong construction, and the two subordinate influences. No single garment family, level of coverage, palette, or cultural appearance becomes a universal template.

#### 6.19.2 Explanation and construction families

The following families are design opportunities. Each must be transformed for the actual character and combined coherently with the active objectives:

- **Open central construction with strong borders:** a narrow front structure, deliberate side openings, a defined neck/upper attachment, and substantial separate arm components. Resolve every support and edge; missing cloth is not an architecture.
- **Asymmetric armor and drape:** one dominant shoulder mass, articulated arm protection, offset waist/hip components, and long controlled fabric masses. Balance the visible torso and thigh regions against the rigid masses.
- **Fitted garment with large sleeves:** a clear torso silhouette with generous sleeve geometry, cuffs, wraps, or separate arm pieces. Sleeve volume must frame the body instead of hiding defining structures.
- **Coat, mantle, or cape over a compact core:** an open front, clear closure and collar, fitted central garment, and layered rear mass. The coat is substantial but cannot conceal anatomical roots or apertures that define identity.
- **Layered robe or panel construction:** split lower panels, broad waist architecture, overlapping borders, cords or structural ties, and deliberate high-cut upper-torso/central-torso opening treatment. Apply the character's frozen actual Era / Culture / Civilization vocabulary visually without assigning a role or historical biography.
- **Structured plate and textile composition:** articulated shoulder/chest/limb plates with a controlled cape or fabric interior. High coverage is available when justified by the resolved architecture, but the open-region objective and defining-anatomy visibility still require explicit treatment.
- **Minimal macro garment with substantial local construction:** a few major garment shapes, controlled openings, strong closures, and crisp material boundaries. Simplicity is a deliberate design, not an unfinished costume.
- **Long ceremonial-looking or monumental silhouette:** large coherent drapes, columns of fabric, collars, and sculpted rigid borders. Translate any aesthetic direction into those concrete features without fictional rank or title.

#### 6.19.3 Worked applications

**Open-sided fitted construction.** A black central torso panel can connect through a defined collar and waist wrap, leaving side anatomy visible; separate forearm pieces and a split lower panel create a complete system. Distinguish all garment edges from body markings. Reinterpret the geometry for the selected character rather than copying a standard outfit.

**Armor with visible abdomen.** A substantial single shoulder plate and articulated gauntlet can be balanced by defined chest boundaries, an open abdomen, a hip frame, split drapes, and tall leg components. The opening map follows the actual body's contours and defining cavities.

**Broad sleeves and layered waist.** A robe-derived outfit can use a fitted upper attachment, large sleeves, an intentionally visible central torso region, layered lower panels, and a strong waist closure. The subordinate visual vocabulary determines the construction of folds, borders, and fasteners, not merely printed motifs.

**Restrained plate/cape construction.** A smooth plate system with a large rear cape can supply strong macro clarity. To remain suitable for a character with a defining flank aperture, the side coverage must be restructured and the cape arranged so the feature remains inspectable. The palette and armor geometry are authored for that character rather than universally blue, white, and gold.

**Translucent and luminous clothing.** Coherent translucent cloth can have layered hems, drawn thickness, and visible attachment. Luminous matter can constitute a collar or panel. Neither material authorizes a surrounding aura, atmospheric trail, or missing garment support.

#### 6.19.4 Failure and validation

An attractive garment that covers the character's distinctive side cavity fails adaptation. A collection with varied trim but the same torso/shoulder/skirt system fails differentiation. A preferred degree of open-region architecture does not authorize changing body proportions. A cultural-looking garment does not authorize symbols, props, or stories unrelated to the assigned visual inputs. Confirm the actual fit, coverage, silhouette, material construction, and concept/sub-concept relationships before generation.

### 6.20 Defining-anatomy visibility map

#### 6.20.1 Rule

For every identity-defining anatomical feature, record its location, visible extent, associated body material, required garment clearance, and how its defining construction remains readable while clothed.

#### 6.20.2 Explanation

Preserving hidden geometry is insufficient when the feature is central to the character's visual identity. Clothing must frame, route around, or provide a dedicated opening for it. This requirement concerns defining features, not leaving every body surface open. Ordinary coverage remains possible wherever it does not erase essential morphology.

#### 6.20.3 Worked application

A flank aperture begins below the ribs and runs obliquely toward the rear hip/lower-torso region. A straight side seam would cover it. Resolve an offset seam, separated front/rear garment boundaries, and supports routed outside the aperture's rim. The visible opening retains its depth, direction, and relationship to the hip/lower-torso region in the final assembled character.

#### 6.20.4 Failure and validation

Reject a strap crossing the defining cavity, a cape burying its outline, false transparency used to pretend it is visible, or a garment that relocates it. Compare the coverage map with the frozen anatomy before rendering and inspect the actual result afterward without a retry.


## 7 PHASE 3 — WEAPON CREATION

Phase 3 creates a dedicated weapon for the already frozen character and frozen outfit. The weapon is independently authored for that character, but its design begins from a simple conventional weapon rather than from an already elaborate fantasy object.

The governing dependency is:

FROZEN CHARACTER + FROZEN OUTFIT → WEAPON-TYPE SELECTION → SIMPLE CONVENTIONAL BASE → CATEGORY READABILITY LOCK → CHARACTER-SPECIFIC STRUCTURAL TRANSFORMATION → MICRO-DETAIL RESOLUTION → PREMIUM-SIGNATURE-WEAPON AESTHETIC PASS → FINAL REFINEMENT / RENDER-ROBUSTNESS CHECK → FREEZE INTERNAL WEAPON SPECIFICATION → CLEAN RENDERER DESCRIPTION → RENDER PREFLIGHT → IMAGE GENERATION → INSPECTION → OUTCOME RECORD → USER SELECTION AND FREEZE

### 7.1 Phase-3 activation gate

Phase 3 begins only on the user’s explicit instruction after the relevant Phase-1 body and Phase-2 outfit have been completely resolved, validated, selected, and frozen.

- The body cannot be redesigned to accommodate the weapon.

- The outfit cannot be redesigned merely because another weapon would be easier to attach or display.

- The weapon must account for the actual character and outfit rather than replacing their design logic.

- Phase 3 adds a weapon system; it does not take ownership of frozen body or clothing systems.

#### 7.1.1 Operational explanation

A weapon is authored only after the user starts Phase 3 and the selected body and outfit are adopted and frozen.

#### 7.1.2 Worked application

A gauntlet with unusual digit clearance constrains grip geometry. The weapon must adapt to it; the hand is not normalized to fit an ordinary handle.

#### 7.1.3 Failure and validation

Check user authorization, frozen IDs/revisions, and complete protected inputs.

### 7.2 Autonomous weapon-type selection

Unless the active task explicitly fixes a weapon category, Phase 3 must select the weapon type that best fits the individual character.

The selection space is open and may include swords, paired swords, greatswords, daggers, paired daggers, spears, lances, halberds, polearms, glaives, scythes, axes, hammers, maces, clubs, bows, crossbows, staves, whips, chains, shields used as primary weapons, hybrid conventional categories, or other coherent recognizable weapon types.

Weapon type must be selected from the resolved character rather than assigned randomly. Consider body scale, proportions, anatomy, appendages, outfit architecture, concept combination, sub-concepts, silhouette, material language, plausible grip/interface, and the visual balance between character and weapon.

Population-level diversity should be maximized when multiple characters receive weapons. Do not default repeatedly to swords merely because swords are convenient. Repetition is allowed only when the repeated category is genuinely the strongest fit; repeated categories must then diverge drastically in their final design.

#### 7.2.1 Operational explanation

Weapon-category selection is a visual fit decision using actual character and outfit construction. It does not infer occupation, fighting history, morality, or narrative role.

#### 7.2.2 Worked application

A candidate population can explore polearms, compact blades, bows, axes, or other recognizable types when their scale and interfaces suit the same character. A user-fixed sword category keeps every candidate a sword.

#### 7.2.3 Failure and validation

Record selected categories and concrete reasons. A default row of near-identical swords fails diversity when no constraint warrants it.

### 7.3 Simple conventional base — absolute rule

Every Phase-3 weapon begins from a simple, ordinary, unworked, immediately recognizable base weapon of the selected category.

The initial base contains only the essential category-defining architecture. Examples:

- Sword: blade, point, edge or edges, ricasso/tang transition where applicable, guard, grip, pommel.

- Spear or lance: head, socket or attachment, shaft, butt/end treatment.

- Halberd or polearm: simple head with category-defining blade/spike geometry, shaft, grip region, butt.

- Dagger: compact blade, point, edge geometry, guard where applicable, grip, pommel.

- Bow: clear limbs, grip/riser, string, conventional bow silhouette.

- Scythe: recognizable curved blade, blade-to-shaft junction, shaft, grip regions.

- Axe: recognizable head, cutting edge, poll/back structure where applicable, haft.

- Hammer or club: recognizable striking head or mass and handle/shaft.

The conventional base is a design scaffold, not the final result. Character-specific complexity is added only after category readability is secured.

#### 7.3.1 Operational explanation

The ordinary base is an internal design scaffold, not a separately generated preliminary image.

#### 7.3.2 Worked application

Begin a sword with blade, edge, point, guard, grip, and terminal. Only after that macro is clear should the design add channels, negative space, insets, segmentation, and material transitions.

#### 7.3.3 Failure and validation

The final blueprint must retain category-defining relationships. An elaborate object with an added handle is not a conventional-base development.

### 7.4 Weapon category readability lock

Once a weapon type is selected, the final weapon must remain unmistakably that type.

Character-specific transformation may radically alter proportions, materials, internal geometry, guard form, apertures, engravings, stones, segmentation, asymmetry, or micro-architecture, but it must not destroy the category-defining macro relationship.

If the silhouette ceases to read immediately as the selected weapon category, the design has exceeded its valid macro complexity and must be simplified.

#### 7.4.1 Operational explanation

Transformation can be extreme inside a recognizable category. Category identity must survive silhouette inspection.

#### 7.4.2 Worked application

A sword may have an asymmetric guard and internal apertures while keeping a readable blade axis, cutting edges, tip, and grip relationship.

#### 7.4.3 Failure and validation

Reject a blade obscured by rings, branches, or unrelated masses so that the selected weapon type no longer reads immediately.

### 7.5 Simple macro / intricate micro — absolute weapon hierarchy

Phase 3 uses an absolute simple-macro / intricate-micro hierarchy.

MACRO means the weapon's primary silhouette, category-defining components, overall proportions, major directional geometry, and largest component relationships. Macro must remain clean, legible, economical, and immediately understandable.

MICRO means the detailed design contained inside or immediately attached to that macro: edge treatment, fullers, channels, engravings, apertures, stones, structural recesses, segmentation, material transitions, small projections, local asymmetry, internal mechanisms, fine ornament, surface patterning, and controlled luminous detail.

Complexity must be concentrated in micro and secondary architecture. It must not be achieved by endlessly adding giant spikes, floating parts, random wings, oversized rings, excessive branching, or unrelated silhouette masses.

A legendary weapon should look sophisticated because every resolved detail reinforces a clean primary form, not because the primary form has become unreadable.

#### 7.5.1 Operational explanation

Simplicity governs primary organization, not the quantity of carefully subordinated internal detail.

#### 7.5.2 Worked application

A long tapered sword can contain fine channels, engraved lines, nested inset borders, and small articulated transitions while retaining a clean blade outline.

#### 7.5.3 Failure and validation

Compare silhouette first, then interior detail. Giant spikes or branching components cannot be counted as micro detail when they dominate the outline.

### 7.5.4 Premium-signature-weapon mandate — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Phase 3 is not “give her a weapon.” The weapon must be authored at flagship gacha/anime/manhwa quality as a signature design inseparable from the character's identity while remaining physically separate from her body and outfit. It must be recognizable as the selected weapon category and simultaneously feel specific enough that showing the weapon alone would strongly evoke its character.

The strongest solution is not necessarily the most complex. Scale, silhouette, category-defining geometry, material, grip/interface, asymmetry, colour, primary-concept echoes, both subordinate influences, and relationship to the outfit/body must work as one design. A generic sword, spear, gun, staff, or other category with theme-coloured decoration fails even when technically polished.

### 7.6 Character-specific weapon authorship

Every weapon must be specifically authored for its actual character. The final weapon should appear inseparable from that character's visual identity even while remaining physically separate from her body and outfit.

Character/weapon relationships may be established through one or more concrete systems:

- proportion and scale;

- geometry;

- material architecture;

- color architecture;

- concept-derived structural logic;

- sub-concept-derived structural logic;

- grip and handling interface;

- body morphology compatibility;

- appendage compatibility;

- outfit attachment or carrying logic;

- asymmetry;

- repeated but transformed shape language;

- controlled echo of body apertures, cavities, segmentation, ridges, plates, or other established structures;

- controlled echo of outfit edges, panels, trims, materials, or major geometric language.

The relationship must be physical and visual. A generic weapon with only a matching color, symbol, glow, or motif is insufficient.

#### 7.6.1 Operational explanation

Establish one or two primary physical relationships to the character, with other secondary relationships where useful. Do not force an exhaustive repetition of every body and outfit motif.

#### 7.6.2 Worked application

Echo the body’s deep oblique aperture construction through the blade’s internal negative space and translate the outfit’s stepped edges into the guard. Resolve scale and grip fit independently.

#### 7.6.3 Failure and validation

Matching colour alone is insufficient. Record substantive geometry/material/interface relationships and preserve physical separation.

### 7.7 Weapon differentiation gate

Weapons must be drastically differentiated between characters, including when two or more characters receive the same category.

For every weapon, resolve as applicable:

- weapon category and subtype;

- overall scale relative to character;

- length, width, thickness, mass impression, and proportion;

- primary silhouette;

- balance and visual center of mass;

- grip count and grip position;

- one-handed, two-handed, paired, or other handling architecture;

- handle length, thickness, cross-section, wrapping, segmentation, and end treatment;

- guard form, guard span, guard thickness, guard curvature, guard asymmetry, and guard-to-blade relationship;

- pommel or terminal geometry;

- blade profile;

- single-edge, double-edge, false-edge, serrated/localized edge, or other coherent edge architecture;

- point/tip geometry;

- blade taper;

- blade curvature;

- ricasso form and length where applicable;

- fullers, grooves, channels, ridges, spines, bevels, or flats;

- aperture count, aperture position, aperture shape, and aperture scale;

- cavities and structural negative space;

- embedded stones, gems, cores, lenses, or analogous inserts;

- engraved, incised, embossed, inlaid, or etched structures;

- segmentation and component joints;

- mechanical or articulated components where applicable;

- material combinations and their boundaries;

- surface treatment;

- transparency, translucency, opacity, gloss, matte behavior, reflectivity, or luminosity where applicable;

- color distribution and accent placement;

- local asymmetry;

- ornament hierarchy;

- micro-detail density.

When the category is not bladed, the same principle applies to the category's actual defining parts. Do not force sword terminology onto bows, clubs, staves, axes, polearms, or other weapon families.

#### 7.7.1 Operational explanation

Every category uses its own actual component vocabulary. A differentiation checklist is not a reason to add irrelevant blade parts to a bow.

#### 7.7.2 Worked application

Two swords can differ in taper, cross-section, edge arrangement, guard architecture, grip proportions, inset organization, and material distribution. Two bows require different limb/riser/string construction instead.

#### 7.7.3 Failure and validation

Compare all 45 pairs in a ten-weapon catalogue and inspect repeated-category clusters.

### 7.8 Same-category anti-template rule

Two swords must not be the same sword with different colors. Two spears must not be the same spear with different heads. Two bows must not be the same bow with different ornaments.

When a category repeats, differentiate the primary internal construction as far as possible while preserving the simple macro:

- different proportion systems;

- different edge or striking geometry;

- different guard/head/riser architecture;

- different handle and grip architecture;

- different negative-space strategy;

- different material architecture;

- different stone/core placement;

- different engraving or channel strategy;

- different symmetry/asymmetry strategy;

- different secondary component relationships;

- different micro-detail language.

Cross-character comparison is mandatory. A new weapon that substantially repeats an occupied weapon architecture must be redesigned before generation.

#### 7.8.1 Operational explanation

Same-category diversity must change internal construction and relationships while preserving simple macro.

#### 7.8.2 Worked application

One sword can use a broad straight blade with a continuous central channel; another a narrow curved blade with offset internal recesses and a compact guard. Their source character still informs both.

#### 7.8.3 Failure and validation

A colour swap, replaced gemstone, or slightly longer handle cannot establish independent candidate architecture.

### 7.9 Mythical / legendary / ominous quality without macro corruption

The final weapon may communicate any fitting high-order visual quality—mythical, legendary, ominous, sacred, ancient, regal, brutal, elegant, alien, corrupted, ceremonial, technological, ethereal, or another character-specific direction—but that quality must emerge from resolved design rather than category destruction.

The workflow must translate such qualities into concrete form, material, proportion, edge, guard, handle, aperture, stone, engraving, surface, asymmetry, and color decisions.

Abstract adjectives are never a substitute for weapon construction.

#### 7.9.1 Operational explanation

High-order aesthetic words are internal design objectives only until translated into physical form. They do not create lore or a fictional title.

#### 7.9.2 Worked application

Translate an imposing direction into measured proportions, a strong guard, a controlled material contrast, and deliberate recessed structure. Do not rely on “legendary” to ask the renderer to invent the weapon.

#### 7.9.3 Failure and validation

Check that every aesthetic objective has concrete component evidence and that category readability remains intact.

### 7.10 Blade-system resolution

For swords, daggers, glaives, scythes, axes, polearms, and other bladed weapons, the cutting or piercing structure must be designed as an actual functional visual system.

- Resolve where the edge begins and ends.

- Resolve edge count.

- Resolve bevel and flat relationships where visually relevant.

- Resolve curvature or straightness.

- Resolve taper and point geometry.

- Resolve spine or back geometry.

- Resolve ricasso or unsharpened transition where applicable.

- Resolve fullers, grooves, channels, apertures, or internal negative space without making the blade unreadable.

- Resolve whether ornament follows structural lines or remains secondary.

- Do not cover the blade with undifferentiated detail soup.

#### 7.10.1 Operational explanation

Resolve actual edge and point relationships even when structural materials are fantastical. Functional visual coherence does not require ordinary realism.

#### 7.10.2 Worked application

A blade aperture has a defined rim and location that preserves the chosen edge path and point geometry. Fine engravings follow the blade’s structural direction.

#### 7.10.3 Failure and validation

Reject missing edges, ambiguous points, fused ornament, or detail covering the entire blade indiscriminately.

### 7.11 Guard, handle, grip, and interface resolution

The grip system must be designed with the same specificity as the blade or weapon head.

- Resolve guard or hand-protection geometry where applicable.

- Resolve handle length and cross-section.

- Resolve wrapping, plating, rings, segmentation, inlays, or other grip construction.

- Resolve pommel or terminal architecture.

- Resolve transitions between weapon body, guard/head, handle, and terminal.

- Resolve how the character actually holds the weapon.

- Account for gloves, gauntlets, unusual hands, claws, extra digits, appendages, or other relevant anatomy.

- Account for outfit sleeves, cuffs, armor, and carrying structures.

- Do not allow decorative complexity to make the grip physically unreadable.

#### 7.11.1 Operational explanation

The weapon’s interface must fit the character’s actual hands and outfit. Grip clarity is a primary component requirement.

#### 7.11.2 Worked application

A long grip accommodates the frozen gloved hand dimensions, with a defined guard clearance and a terminal that does not merge into the sleeve.

#### 7.11.3 Failure and validation

Check handle size, grip placement, transitions, and carrying/holding compatibility without modifying the body or outfit.

### 7.12 Apertures, stones, engravings, and secondary structures

Apertures, embedded stones, engraved structures, cores, channels, and other secondary systems are permitted and encouraged when they improve character specificity, but each must have deliberate placement and hierarchy.

For each such system, resolve count, scale, position, shape, orientation, depth impression, material, border treatment, repetition rhythm, and relationship to the weapon's primary geometry.

Secondary structures must reinforce the weapon's flow. They cannot become arbitrary decoration scattered over the surface.

#### 7.12.1 Operational explanation

Every secondary detail needs a location, construction, and hierarchy. Decorative scatter is not sophistication.

#### 7.12.2 Worked application

Place a circular inset inside a defined guard/blade junction, frame it with nested borders, and align subsidiary channels with the blade axis. A different candidate must choose a different organization.

#### 7.12.3 Failure and validation

Check counts, rims, depth, orientation, material, and connection. Detached orbiting insets are not structural detail.

### 7.13 Weapon material integrity

When a material is structural, it must visibly constitute the weapon's underlying construction.

Metal, crystal, stone-like material, bone-like material, glass-like material, translucent matter, luminous matter, liquid-like coherent matter, mechanical composite, or other permitted material must be represented through the weapon's actual geometry and illustrated material behavior.

A structural material cannot be reduced to a coating, aura, glow, decorative overlay, or detached external effect.

#### 7.13.1 Operational explanation

Materials constitute the weapon, rather than being lighting effects painted over an unresolved shape.

#### 7.13.2 Worked application

A glass-like blade has clear illustrated thickness and internal boundaries; bone-like matter forms actual struts; luminous matter occupies a bounded component.

#### 7.13.3 Failure and validation

Reject a generic blade whose only material specificity is glow, a surface shader, or a detached aura.

### 7.14 Weapon/body/outfit separation

The weapon must remain a physically distinct weapon.

- It must not accidentally become an anatomical appendage.

- It must not become a garment panel.

- It must not merge ambiguously into armor.

- It must not become an environmental object.

- It must not rely on detached effects to define its basic shape.

- Wearable integration or attachment is allowed only when the weapon itself remains clearly separable and readable.

#### 7.14.1 Operational explanation

A weapon can attach to clothing or be carried, but remains a distinct system with a clear interface.

#### 7.14.2 Worked application

A sheathed blade connects through an outfit attachment while the blade, sheath, fastening, and body remain individually readable. A grip held in a hand does not fuse into the fingers.

#### 7.14.3 Failure and validation

Reject weapons becoming appendages, garment panels, environmental architecture, or undefined effects.

### 7.15 Scale and silhouette relationship

Weapon scale must be resolved relative to the actual character. Large weapons are allowed; small weapons are allowed; paired weapons are allowed. Scale must serve the character-specific design rather than a universal preference.

The combined character-plus-weapon silhouette must remain readable. The weapon should contribute a strong secondary identity without visually replacing the character.

#### 7.15.1 Operational explanation

Scale is tailored to the individual character and presentation; oversized is not the universal default.

#### 7.15.2 Worked application

A broad greatsword may balance one selected body, while a compact paired system suits another. Each must preserve character dominance and a complete visible weapon.

#### 7.15.3 Failure and validation

Check relative dimensions, mass impression, holding clearance, and full extent without cropping.

### 7.16 Phase-3 2D-native rendering lock

Phase 3 is strictly 2D and inherits the same 2D-native rendering discipline as every other phase.

- Use clean illustrated contours and controlled line hierarchy.

- Keep primary weapon geometry graphically readable.

- Use drawn highlights, drawn material transitions, and controlled 2D shading.

- Represent metal and other reflective materials without PBR or CGI rendering.

- Avoid photorealistic texture, plastic rendering, game-render appearance, volumetric 3D material simulation, and pseudo-photographic reflections.

- Do not allow 3D rendering to soften, distort, thicken, melt, or otherwise destroy deliberately resolved edge, guard, aperture, engraving, or handle geometry.

- Micro-detail must remain crisp enough to read as intentional drawn construction.

#### 7.16.1 Operational explanation

The embedded 2D grammar must preserve precise weapon geometry. Drawn reflection is permitted; 3D material simulation is not.

#### 7.16.2 Worked application

Use crisp bevel highlights and controlled dark face shapes on a black blade. Keep the aperture rim and guard edges exact instead of softening them into a reflective blur.

#### 7.16.3 Failure and validation

Inspect edge, inset, engraving, and handle clarity independently from overall visual appeal.

### 7.17 Phase-3 design resolution gate

No weapon may be sent to generation until all applicable design systems are resolved:

- weapon type selection;

- simple conventional base;

- category-defining macro geometry;

- scale and proportions;

- character-specific relationship;

- blade/head/limb/shaft/riser architecture as appropriate to category;

- guard and handle/interface architecture where applicable;

- edge and point geometry where applicable;

- apertures and negative space;

- stones, cores, engravings, channels, or analogous secondary structures;

- material architecture;

- color architecture;

- asymmetry;

- macro simplicity;

- micro-detail hierarchy;

- 2D rendering translation;

- frozen-body and frozen-outfit preservation.

If the weapon is merely described as 'legendary', 'ominous', 'mythical', 'cool', 'badass', or another abstract quality without the physical decisions that produce that quality, the weapon is unresolved and cannot proceed to generation.

#### 7.17.1 Operational explanation

Every applicable structural decision and every protected input must be resolved before Frozen Specification / Clean Renderer-Facing Description certification.

#### 7.17.2 Worked application

An unresolved guard-to-grip transition or an unassigned material boundary blocks generation even if the silhouette is attractive.

#### 7.17.3 Failure and validation

Require evidence, not adjectives. Recheck any changed macro or interface decision before the call.

### 7.17A Phase-3 deterministic weapon blueprint — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Before any Phase-3 READY TO RENDER certification, materialize each weapon candidate as one complete deterministic weapon blueprint. Internally resolve, as applicable: frozen body/outfit interface assumptions; weapon category and category-defining macro silhouette; overall dimensions/ratios where visually or functionally important; complete component inventory and counts; primary blade/head/shaft/limb/riser geometry; guard/grip/handle/terminal/connectors; edge/point/taper/curvature/section profile; articulation or segmentation; apertures/channels/insets/cores/engravings; material allocation, thickness, joinery and stiffness/flexibility; colour topology; asymmetry/repeated-unit logic; character-specific adaptation; carrying/grip/attachment interface; and enough multi-angle information to fix hidden/rear relationships.

The blueprint fixes one exact object. Category labels or evocative adjectives never substitute for geometry. A competent independent illustrator using the blueprint must reconstruct the same macro silhouette, components, interfaces, material boundaries, colour topology, articulation and carrying relation without choosing among materially different alternatives.

**Internal/renderer boundary.** The internal weapon blueprint may be detailed. The Clean Renderer-Facing Description and FINAL RENDER CONTEXT remain shortest-lossless visible weapon constructions with no word-count minimum. Preserve exact dimensions/counts where they materially define silhouette, function, interfaces, articulation or identity; keep audit bookkeeping and below-scale proof detail internal. The renderer never invents missing major weapon geometry. This additive blueprint never changes Phase-3 ownership: the frozen body and outfit are immutable inputs and only the weapon is authored.

### 7.18 Phase-3 generator handoff

Phase 3 enters generator handoff only after the weapon is complete final-refined render-ready state. Freeze the exact weapon revision internally; public written weapon specification is optional and shown only on request.

Compile a positive renderer-facing description that preserves the frozen character and outfit plus the weapon's exact category, macro proportions, blade/head geometry, guard/handle/grip system, attachment and carrying relationship, materials, apertures/stones/engravings where selected, and character-specific scale relationship. The renderer does not invent weapon type, category, missing geometry, or attachment logic.

Run the renderer preflight for category readability, silhouette, grip/carry relationship, body/outfit separation, full extents, concise positive serialization, and description clarity. Then run the Renderer Capability Gate. A SUPPORTED result enters READY TO RENDER; image submission still requires an explicit user `RENDER` command.
#### 7.18.1 Operational explanation

The internal weapon design may remain exhaustive. The renderer-facing block preserves only render-critical construction and material relationships at the image scale.

#### 7.18.2 Worked application

A resolved sword keeps its exact blade proportion, fuller/channel structure, guard architecture, grip length, pommel, material segmentation, and scale against the frozen character. The renderer-facing block states those structures directly without reopening weapon design.

#### 7.18.3 Failure and validation

If the preflight finds category ambiguity, missing grip logic, silhouette collision, omitted material boundaries, or contradictory scale, correct the frozen weapon specification or serializer before submission. Post-render deviations remain attempt evidence.

### 7.19 Phase-3 post-generation audit

After generation, inspect the actual weapon.

- Is the selected weapon category immediately recognizable?

- Did the macro remain simple and clean?

- Did the generator preserve the intended proportions?

- Are blade/head, guard, handle, shaft, grip, and terminal structures correct where applicable?

- Are edge and point geometries correct?

- Are apertures, stones, engravings, channels, and negative spaces in the intended locations?

- Are structural materials actually structural?

- Does the weapon look specifically designed for this character?

- Is it drastically differentiated from other weapons in the population?

- Did it preserve the frozen body and outfit?

- Is micro-detail subordinate to macro geometry?

- Is the result genuinely 2D?

- Did 3D-like rendering distort or erase deliberate geometry?

- Did the generator invent giant silhouette masses, excessive spikes, floating parts, or unrelated effects that were not part of the resolved design?

Failure of a mandatory property invalidates the generated weapon. Record FAILED, preserve the image unchanged, and perform no correction or retry. Continue only with remaining authorized work in the active phase.

#### 7.19.1 Operational explanation

Inspect the generated weapon against the actual package and record its immutable outcome.

#### 7.19.2 Worked application

If the rendered sword loses its point, moves the inset, or becomes a 3D-looking object, record FAILED. Do not correct and regenerate.

#### 7.19.3 Failure and validation

Preserve the attempt and continue only already-authorized work. A post-audit explanation does not fulfil a missing requirement.

### 7.20 Phase-3 certification and freeze

A weapon becomes frozen only after passing the Phase-3 design-resolution gate and post-generation audit and explicit user adoption and freeze.

Certification freezes the weapon category, subtype, macro silhouette, proportions, primary components, grip/interface, major geometry, material architecture, color architecture, identity-defining apertures/stones/engravings, asymmetry, and characteristic micro-detail.

The frozen weapon becomes an immutable Phase-4 input unless the user explicitly requests a targeted revision.

#### 7.20.1 Operational explanation

A weapon is frozen only after passing inspection and explicit user adoption. Selection or freeze does not end the phase.

#### 7.20.2 Worked application

The user can select two weapons, fuse their construction, alter the grip, and then freeze the result, all within Phase 3.

#### 7.20.3 Failure and validation

Record the chosen revision and exact body/outfit dependencies before assembly.

### 7.21 Phase-2 / Phase-3 dependency invariant

The complete design dependency is:

CONCEPT COMBINATION → PHASE-1 BODY → FROZEN CHARACTER → PHASE-2 OUTFIT → FROZEN OUTFIT → PHASE-3 WEAPON → FROZEN WEAPON → PHASE-4 ASSEMBLY

Each later phase adds its assigned system while preserving all certified earlier systems. A later phase may adapt to an earlier phase but may not silently redesign it.

#### 7.21.1 Operational explanation

Dependency order establishes ownership, not automatic progression. The user begins and ends each phase.

#### 7.21.2 Worked application

A completed weapon remains available for further Phase-3 development until the user requests Phase 4. Assembly cannot conceal or redesign earlier defining anatomy.

#### 7.21.3 Failure and validation

Check upstream frozen revisions, explicit phase command, and preservation of all earlier systems.

### 7.22 Ten-candidate weapon catalogue

#### 7.22.1 Rule

An initial weapon catalogue contains ten structurally distinct candidate weapon systems for the selected frozen character and outfit, arranged in two rows of five on one continuous 16:9 plain-white sheet. Display complete isolated weapons, not newly posed characters or scenes.

#### 7.22.2 Explanation

The catalogue shares the outfit catalogue's organizational principle. Long weapons may use an economical diagonal orientation within their allocated space. Each item remains fully visible and visually separate. A paired weapon system counts as one candidate when explicitly designed as a pair; display both members coherently. Category-specific strings, joints, heads, guards, handles, terminals, and carrying components included in the design must not be cropped or silently omitted.

Minimal candidate numbers support selection. Do not add narrative epithets, floating effects, ornamental backgrounds, sample material boards, or a character holding each item unless explicitly requested. Intrinsic luminous material remains bounded to the actual weapon. Physical separation for catalogue display is legitimate weapon presentation, not an exception allowing external concept effects.

#### 7.22.3 Worked application

A sword fixed by the user as the required category yields ten different swords. Candidate 01 may use a broad straight blade with deep central negative space; Candidate 02 may use a slender curved blade with different guard, grip, taper, and material organization. Continue with independently resolved architectures rather than ten colours of one sword. If no category is fixed, choose suitable distinct types from the open category space while preserving character fit.

#### 7.22.4 Failure and validation

Check all 45 pair comparisons, complete extent, category readability, grip/interface, material structure, colour architecture, simple macro, intricate subordinate detail, and 2D rendering. A black or transparent-looking field is not the required plain white background. No emitted tendrils or auras may carry the concept outside the weapon.

### 7.23 Detailed sword construction example

#### 7.23.1 Rule

A sword may be highly distinctive through its internal architecture while retaining an immediately legible blade–guard–grip–terminal relationship. Its complexity must reinforce the sword, not replace it with a decorative mass.

#### 7.23.2 Explanation

One useful construction investigation combines a long tapered blade, clear cutting edges, a defined central channel, a substantial guard, a resolved grip, and an inset at the guard/blade junction. The inset is a physical component with rim, depth, material, and connection. Channels, recesses, fine branching internal supports, and drawn luminous matter may relate it to the blade's structure. None requires an orbiting disk or detached energy.

#### 7.23.3 Worked application

Begin from a simple two-edged sword. Resolve overall length, blade-to-grip ratio, straight axis, taper, point, edge paths, guard span, grip cross-section, and terminal. Then introduce an asymmetric but readable internal channel system, a bounded circular inset with a nested rim near the guard, and a contrasting blade-edge material. Define a small number of subsidiary engraved or recessed lines following the blade axis. Translate the selected character's cavity logic into the inset's physical border, and its outfit geometry into the guard transitions. Draw the result with sharp illustrated bevel highlights, controlled face values, and exact internal edges.

#### 7.23.4 Alternative application

A dark narrow blade can instead use an openwork guard, a clear inset at its center, and fine structural struts that follow the main blade. Wispy surrounding matter is not part of the weapon. If tendril-like geometry is used, it must be a defined attached component with clear hierarchy and must not obscure the blade's primary outline.

#### 7.23.5 Failure and validation

Do not impose this sword's inset location, length, palette, guard, or blade organization on every future weapon. Do not infer that every weapon needs a gem or aperture. A repeated sword with a new core colour fails candidate independence. A blade hidden behind large rings or enormous projections fails simple macro. A beautiful PBR-looking sword fails the 2D rule. Compare physical construction and character-specific relationships before the one generation call.


## 8 PHASE 4 — FINAL ASSEMBLY

### 8.1 Activation and immutable inputs

**Rule.** Phase 4 begins only on the user's explicit instruction after the selected body, outfit, and weapon have passing, adopted, frozen states. Assembly preserves their exact identity and construction.

**Explanation.** Phase 4 combines completed systems. It does not invent missing body features, redesign clothing, choose an unresolved weapon, or fix an earlier failed image. The source of truth is the selected frozen revision of each system and their verified dependency relationship.

**Application.** A chosen body with a flank aperture, an outfit tailored around that aperture, and a specifically designed sword are assembled as one finished character. Every source system retains its proportions, material architecture, colours, topology, attachments, and defining details.

**Failure and validation.** Reject stale inputs, missing fit information, substituted body proportions, altered face/hair, changed clothing coverage, or a different sword. Record all three frozen IDs and their compatibility before resolving presentation.

### 8.2 Single-character 9:16 analytical assembly sheet

**Rule.** The default Phase-4 assembly output is one exact 9:16 analytical character sheet containing the complete selected character with the frozen outfit and weapon on plain white: one dominant true-front full-body view above, one smaller exact 90-degree profile below-left, and one smaller direct-rear view below-right, using the same identity-preservation hierarchy as the Phase-1 single-character analytical format. If the user explicitly asks for a portrait rather than an analytical sheet, that request overrides presentation only; frozen body, outfit and weapon remain unchanged.

**Explanation.** Full extent includes body, hair, appendages, garments, and weapon in every required view. A natural static holding arrangement may position the weapon clearly without obscuring defining anatomy. Use economical white clearance and controlled character-centric composition. No environment, throne, floor scenery, celestial disk, atmospheric field, or external conceptual effect is added.

**Application.** Preserve the same frozen character, garment construction, appendage roots/counts, body topology, markings, palette and weapon geometry across front, profile and rear. Long garment masses and the weapon remain fully inside the canvas with white clearance margin.

**Supporting panels.** No filler characters are added. Small supporting details may appear only when explicitly useful, genuinely fit unused negative space, and do not reduce or crop the required three views. They reproduce frozen details rather than introducing new designs.

**Failure and validation.** A detailed environment, dramatic angle that replaces an orthographic view, cropped distal structure, changed rear construction, missing weapon tip, or inconsistent front/profile/rear identity fails. Check exact 9:16 ratio, complete extent, three-view hierarchy, white field, frozen-state fidelity, and cross-view reconstruction.
### 8.3 Visible preservation of defining anatomy

**Rule.** The assembled clothing and weapon must preserve the visible defining morphology of the frozen body. Technical existence beneath clothing is insufficient.

**Explanation.** A central identity feature must remain readable through appropriate garment geometry and object placement. Do not change the aperture, body, or frozen garment during assembly. If their certified relationship is unresolved, the assembly gate is blocked; later-stage presentation cannot silently correct the earlier design.

**Application.** A deep side opening is kept clear by the already-resolved garment boundaries. A hanging panel is placed according to its frozen attachment and drape so it does not cross the aperture. The sword rests on the other side if that is compatible with its resolved interface and the user's command.

**Failure and validation.** Reject an outfit that hides the opening, a weapon that blocks it without a clear readable presentation, or a body redesigned to make the final silhouette easier. Compare feature location, visibility, material, shape, depth, and connection against frozen state.

### 8.3.1 Flagship final-character presentation — HARD / ABSOLUTE

Phase 4 presents the completed character as one coherent premium design. It must preserve the body, outfit, and weapon while selecting composition, scale, and visual hierarchy that make their combined identity feel iconic rather than merely assembled. The result should function as flagship promotional character art without redesigning any frozen system.

### 8.4 Assembly hierarchy and 2D material continuity

**Rule.** The character remains the primary visual identity. Outfit and weapon are character-specific subordinate systems. All materials use the same coherent embedded 2D drawing grammar.

**Explanation.** Rich internal detail, long drapes, reflective blades, and unusual body matter can coexist through clear major masses and controlled interior detail. The render must not become a 3D asset because assembly increases complexity. Drawn gloss and gradients remain permissible when intentionally illustrated.

**Application.** Keep a strong central body silhouette, a few coherent garment masses, and one clearly separated weapon axis. Use crisp drawn material boundaries between body plates, garment layers, and blade. Fine etched or scaled detail follows those primary forms.

**Failure and validation.** Reject identity replaced by a costume, a weapon dominating the entire character, indistinguishable body/clothing materials, or CGI/PBR-looking response. Check body, garment, and weapon independently and together.

### 8.5 Assembly package, description handoff, result, and continuation

Resolve the exact complete assembly before generation. Freeze the selected body, outfit, weapon, materials, attachments, defining-anatomy visibility, pose, camera, white-field presentation, and complete silhouette as one internal Final Assembly Specification.

Written assembly output is optional and shown only when the user asks to inspect it. Derive the Clean Renderer-Facing Description from the frozen assembly, run the same renderer preflight, and run the Renderer Capability Gate. A SUPPORTED result enters READY TO RENDER; only an explicit user `RENDER` command authorizes the image call.

The renderer-facing assembly description uses conventional positive character-art language and preserves all immutable upstream systems. It names the actual character, frozen outfit components, weapon geometry, attachments, pose, and materials rather than internal body/outfit audit objectives. It does not reopen body, outfit, or weapon design and does not ask the renderer to invent missing assembly relationships.

Inspect the actual result against the frozen assembly and record the outcome. A post-render image deviation does not alter the frozen body, outfit, or weapon revisions.
## 9 VALIDATION

The actual image is compared against the certified package. All rendered-result checks in this section are diagnostic pass/fail checks, never permission for post-render revision.

### 9.1 Phase scope and evidence

Phase 1 audits each of the three initial five-character sheets under the clean mandatory-three-view character-reference contract below; the three sheets belong to one fifteen-character population. Phase 2 uses the ten-outfit catalogue and frozen-body preservation contract. Phase 3 uses the ten-weapon catalogue and frozen-body/outfit preservation contract. Phase 4 uses the exact 9:16 analytical assembled-character front/profile/rear contract. Each phase follows its own positive presentation contract, plain white background, embedded 2D grammar, and faithful frozen-state preservation.
